# What a student is told

116 learnings. Each says where it came from and what argues against it. [What a perfect question is](../PERFECT-QUESTION.md) collects the criteria by facet, and [How to generate perfect questions](../GENERATING.md) collects the methods by stage.

### A general rule holds for every case

A rule a lesson presents as true in general must hold for every case the lesson itself teaches. One lesson's closing sentence was false for two of the five cases it taught.

A reading lesson asked students to recite that every part keeps the plant alive, which is false for flowers and seeds, two of the five parts it taught, and contradicts the lesson's own rubric and closing text. The closing-questions spec requires the sentence to be true of every story or text, and the review notes that no mechanical check could tell this sentence was false; it took subject knowledge.

Quoted:

- `shadow-content-eval/reports/G3-U2-L1-review.md` (23): “**1. p24c `name-it` — the threshold sentence is false for two of the five parts the lesson teaches.**”
- `shadow-content-eval/reports/G3-U2-L1-review.md` (31): “- So a child who has understood p21 is being asked to recite a sentence that contradicts it.”
- `shadow-content-eval/reports/G3-U2-L1-review.md` (280): “- **Q1's truth.** No mechanical check can tell that "every part keeps the plant alive" is false for reproductive parts. That needs subject knowledge.”
- `shadow-content-eval/references/closing_questions_pattern.md` (35): “it is true of every story or text, not just this plot;”
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-08/all-grades-source-audit/GRADE4.md` (62): “At p6a, caption says trouble falls once the choice is made; the diagram peaks at choice and falls to return. In this story, the crew's choice creates a new disaster rather than resolving it.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/video/u1-script-and-visual-plan.md` (370-372): “"epic after epic" is an indefinite-repetition idiom a nine-year-old hears as "all of them", which is the universal claim research section 6 rejects, just phrased more carefully.”
- `g9/sources/Year 9 ELA_ Lesson Design Specification for Devfactory.md` (68): “The characters never hear this. We do. That is dramatic irony: the audience knows something the characters don't.”
- `g9/sources/Othello Unit Video_ Info Sheet for Devfactory.md` (45): “Othello never hears this. We do. That is dramatic irony: the audience knows something a character doesn't.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-4/feedback-on-iteration-4/feedback-0806-pedagogy.md` (40): “the round-1/round-2 unclosed overclaim "Every story we read has the same five-step Story Pattern"”

Against: none found

### A guide is not a chatbot

Don't build help or independent work on a text as a chat thread: independent practice should produce the learner's own writing in a structured frame, not conversation turns. The chat pattern had been raised 4 times across two units when the review made replacing it a standing requirement.

The review of a Grade 9 lesson prototype met the chat pattern for the fourth time across two units, this time in independent practice, where the student should be writing alone (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, line 34), and made a guide that is not a chatbot a standing requirement (line 36). Its build checklist replaces the chat thread and turns the guide's redirect copy into per-row hints, available on request, inside the stem grid (lines 123-124). Notes from a meeting the next day record dropping the chat-based approach after feedback that it failed to support learning (`g9/library/text/L64fb8b.txt`, line 15).

Quoted:

- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (36): “The guide is a peer, a pal, an instructor — not a chatbot. Typing an answer into a conversation and receiving a reply is the wrong model of what a child is doing when they study a text, and it is the wrong message about where answers come from.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (34): “This has now been raised four times across the two units, and in this prototype it appears in independent practice”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (123): “Replace the chat thread.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (124): “Convert the Archivist’s redirect copy into per-row hints, available on request, inside the stem grid.”
- `g9/library/text/L64fb8b.txt` (15): “Discarded the chat-based approach after feedback indicated it failed to support student learning.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (36): “The guide is a peer, a pal, an instructor — not a chatbot. Typing an answer into a conversation and receiving a reply is the wrong model of what a child is doing when they study a text, and it is the wrong message about where answers come from.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (34): “the point in the lesson where the student should be producing writing on their own, which makes a chat turn the furthest thing from the deliverable.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (123): “Replace the chat thread.”
- `g9/sources/meeting-notes/Incept Service CoE Catchup - 2026_10_07 10_30 CEST - Notes by Gemini.md` (15): “Discarded the chat-based approach after feedback indicated it failed to support student learning.”

Against: Whether a guide replying in prose still counts as chat was left open: "whether an instructor figure replying in prose satisfies the "peer, pal, instructor" requirement, given that the chat pattern was rejected." (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, line 378). A build meeting described what the chat was for: "So in some cases u the student answers something and then he will ask a follow-up question to probe further and through that they basically deepen their knowledge or add perspectives to their own thought." (`g9/sources/meeting-notes/Incept Service CoE Catchup - 2026_10_07 10_30 CEST - Notes by Gemini.md`, line 310). The record `dialogue-and-refutation-beat-exposition` prefers explaining through dialogue where there is time; that concerns explanation, not the learner's own work on a text.

### A guide welcomes, never gatekeeps

Write a guide's lines as a welcome, never as a gatekeeper withholding answers, and fix a patronising guide by changing its stance toward the learner, not its words. The rejected intro line was 'I won't hand you the answers. I'll make you earn them'; its replacement says readers have followed this story for four hundred years.

Feedback on a Grade 9 unit film called its guide's intro, "I won't hand you the answers. I'll make you earn them", a young gatekeeper stance, and patronising. The voice direction was rewritten as an elder glad a new reader has come, warm and never patronising, the film's closing scene gained a line of welcome, and the design has the check-card copy follow the declarative feedback lines rather than the intro. This is one review's verdict; there are no learner data on how teenagers hear either stance.

Quoted:

- `g9/player/design/ARCHIVIST-DESIGN.md` (177): “The *intro* lines are the problem: "I won't hand you the answers. I'll make you earn them" is the young gatekeeper stance she calls patronising (4.2).”
- `g9/player/design/ARCHIVIST-DESIGN.md` (177): “**the feedback lines are the best copy in the build** — declarative, one fact, no praise inflation, no exclamation marks, every wrong answer corrected with the reason.”
- `g9/player/design/ARCHIVIST-DESIGN.md` (177): “Card copy should follow the feedback lines, not the intro.”
- `g9/script/v02/CHANGES.md` (19): “Elder storyteller: wisdom of time and knowledge, glad a new reader has come; warm and grave, never patronizing; ominous only where the words turn dark.”
- `g9/script/v02/CHANGES.md` (14): “Readers have followed this story for four hundred years. Now it is your turn.”
- `g9/player/audio-masters/archivist-sulafat/archivist-manifest.json` (4): “Speak directly to one young reader who has just paused the film: melancholy and watchful, but not slow; short, clear sentences; a guide, not the show; warm but never patronizing.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (184): “At times the character feels patronizing again, and I don’t like that she’s young.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (69): “Register: the wisdom of time and knowledge, plus the excitement that a new reader has arrived.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (190): “The mechanism behind "patronizing" is stance rather than vocabulary.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (190): “A young guide explaining things to a child reads as condescension between peers; an elder sharing something they consider precious reads as initiation.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (190): “The same sentence can do either depending on who is saying it and why, so the fix is about the relationship the character is in, not about simplifying or complicating the words.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (13): “Anything under "Note" is an observation made during the review rather than part of that feedback — it is offered as rationale and has not been confirmed.”

- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (190): “A young guide explaining things to a child reads as condescension between peers; an elder sharing something they consider precious reads as initiation.”

- `g9/library/text/L96a053.txt` (177): “The *intro* lines are the problem: "I won't hand you the answers. I'll make you earn them" is the young gatekeeper stance she calls patronising (4.2).”

Against: The stance explanation is a review note, which the review says is rationale that has not been confirmed (`g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md`, line 13), and the recast is still marked open in its checklist (line 69).

### A guide who mentors at set moments

Make an on-screen guide an encouraging mentor more expert than the learner, not a peer, shown only at a few key moments and, for teenagers, never cartoonish or over-praising. In the study behind the rule, an experiment with 71 college students found the mentor agent led to overall improved learning and motivation.

A Grade 9 lesson design specification gives its guide character a mentor's role in independent practice, prompting and giving sentence stems so that students do the hard thinking, and lists among the things the guide never does appearing constantly or interrupting unprompted, over-praising and a cartoonish tone. Its research basis cites the authors of a study on pedagogical agents, with no year; their 2005 study compared expert, motivator and mentor agents with college students. The course brief for a 3D version keeps the guide to set moments only, never inside a reading section. The specification also holds that a guide for teenagers should never be cartoonish or over-praising, saying teens reject both.

Quoted:

- `othello-archive/sources/Year 9 ELA- Lesson Design Specification for Devfactory.txt` (477): “Guides in a mentor role outperform expert-only and motivator-only guides (Baylor & Kim). Teens reject cartoonish or over-praising guides. Guides should appear at set moments, not constantly.”
- `othello-archive/sources/Year 9 ELA- Lesson Design Specification for Devfactory.txt` (312): “a side-panel mentor that prompts and gives sentence stems.”

- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g7-science/brainlifts/video-visuals-human-v0.1.md` (195-196): “a face competes with a dynamic scientific visual when both occupy the channel.”

Published: [Baylor and Kim 2005, Simulating instructional roles through pedagogical agents, International Journal of Artificial Intelligence in Education 15, 95-115](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3233/IRG-2005-15%282%2902) · [Baylor & Kim 2005, Simulating Instructional Roles through Pedagogical Agents](http://createcenter.net/PDFs/2005_SimulatingInstructionalRoles.pdf) · [Kim & Baylor 2006](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11423-006-8805-z) · [Beege et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.edurev.2023.100564)

Against: The specification gives the study no year, DOI or URL, and its claim that teens reject cartoonish or over-praising guides cites no source. In the 2005 study each role helped something different: the expert led to more information acquisition and the motivator to more self-efficacy, so 'outperform' simplifies it. Nothing here measured the guide's effect on these learners. The project's citation check flagged the study's DOI: "1 malformed DOI (Baylor & Kim 2005, file 02)." (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/VIDEO-SYNTHESIS.md`, line 10). The two notes grade the study differently, "two controlled experiments, moderate N" in one (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/video-02-instructional-video-design.md`, lines 49-50) and observational or quasi-experimental in the other (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/idea-02-ai-classmates-multi-agent-debate.md`, line 18), and its participants were college students. The evidence is mixed, from small adult samples, and was seen only as a search summary (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md`, lines 70 and 71); the less competent agent's gain in self-efficacy argues for a peer where confidence is the aim. The review warns against removing the guide entirely: "Do not go to zero, though." (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, line 179), and the project's video research agrees: "**Voice-only or text-only is not the right default**, even though it removes all rendering risk." (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/video-02-instructional-video-design.md`, line 148). The reasons come from review notes, which the review says are rationale that has not been confirmed (`g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md`, line 13). The built prototype shows the guide's avatar throughout, though not as video (`g9/sources/meeting-notes/Incept Service CoE Catchup - 2026_10_07 10_30 CEST - Notes by Gemini.md`, line 278). The guide reports no pooled effect size for presence, calls the timing of a face over a diagram a production decision, and counts its open and close durations as operating heuristics (`grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g7-science/brainlifts/video-visuals-human-v0.1.md`, lines 201-203 and 335).

### A planned follow-up for every answer

Plan the follow-up for each kind of answer in advance: challenge a full answer further, hold level on a brief one, and step support down for the rest. One built lesson routes each open answer to one of five paths, and a silent child is taught first, then asked again more easily.

Two sources reach the same branches. A research-based conversation table for early reading scaffolds up on a full answer, holds level on a correct but brief one, and scaffolds down with feedback, reframing, an answer or a model when a child struggles (Zucker et al. 2020). A built Grade 3 lesson's standing dialogue rule routes open answers to full, brief, partial, off-track and silent paths, each of which teaches, and teaches a silent child first before asking again more easily.

Quoted:

- `downloads/WF - Brainlift Opportunities to - 260724-110911.md` (90): “When a child answers easily, scaffold up by challenging for reasoning, alternatives, generalizing, or extending. When a child struggles, scaffold down by giving feedback, reframing, supplying an answer, offering a model to mimic, or simply keeping the child engaged (Zucker et al., 2020).”
- `downloads/WF - Brainlift Opportunities to - 260724-110911.md` (99): “Correct but brief: “She lost her mitten.” | Level”
- `downloads/SUPERSEDED — see v2.1_ WITW Lesson 1 learning walkthrough v2 (table format).md` (47): “Open answers route to full / brief / partial / off-track / silent paths | Every path teaches; the silent child is taught first, then re-asked easier”

Published: [Zucker et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1002/trtr.1943)

Against: The research table plans the kinds of move in advance but composes the words in the moment ("Decide the follow-up rule in advance, but compose the follow-up in the moment."), while the built lesson routes each answer to a set path. A later run found: A grade-3 rulebook rules that full credit carries no further ask: a passed answer never shows a push such as 'Make it even stronger' on its find, write-up, short-response, say-back, retell and check surfaces, which bars challenging a full answer further wherever the answer is scored (`grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/01-NON-NEGOTIABLES.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/01-NON-NEGOTIABLES.md`).

### A reason to read, kept in view

Before students start, give them one purpose for reading that points at the evidence the later task will use, and keep the lesson's question and steps on screen.

A review of grade-3 showcase lessons found a lesson with no stated purpose for reading and asked for a reason to read before the children start; it also found the modelling steps and the live question scrolling away, so the child lost the thread, and asked that both stay visible with one thing surfaced at a time. The Year 9 lesson design specification gives every lesson one learning question that every phase builds toward, annotated and pinned to the top of every screen. The purpose must not teach the later task's answer, and the current steps stay on screen while students work.

### A recap of the worked example

In independent practice, let students open a recap of what the worked example did at each step, drawn from the example's own material rather than the current answer.

A version-3 inference lesson design for a Grade 3 course gives every independent step a collapsible recap of what was done in the modelled step, which refers back to the worked example on an earlier section of the story, plus contextual help (`downloads/5. Curriculum/Instruction/3.1 - Fantasy Classics - Lesson Designs/3.1 - 2. Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp, Part 2 (FICTION, INFER) — v3 (new INFER design).docx`, lines 6 and 213 of its text). The first step's recap describes the process the model followed, reading the question and seeing that the story never says why, with no answer for the new section (line 217). No outcome was measured.

Quoted:

- `downloads/5. Curriculum/Instruction/3.1 - Fantasy Classics - Lesson Designs/3.1 - 2. Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp, Part 2 (FICTION, INFER) — v3 (new INFER design).docx` (213 (textutil text)): “Every step carries a collapsible … recap that references the cave model, plus contextual help.”
- `downloads/5. Curriculum/Instruction/3.1 - Fantasy Classics - Lesson Designs/3.1 - 2. Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp, Part 2 (FICTION, INFER) — v3 (new INFER design).docx` (6 (textutil text)): “every Independent (Phase 3) step now carries a toggleable … recap that references the cave model”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/qc-2026-09-29-staging/ADJUDICATION-pr662.md` (16): “Replaced carryover London/Lost Boys summaries with the current Neverland/Hook fit-word summaries.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/signoff-packet/CROSS-FIELD-SCAN-2026-08-31.md` (99-102): “A child who taps the hint mid-task, without holding the "watch me think" page in memory, will read "gloomy, miserable, strict" or "Lost Boys... gentle, lonely" while looking at a Neverland/Hook question and can easily mistake it for a hint about their OWN answer”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/04-CONTENT-AUTHORING.md` (185): “A hint must always coach the CURRENT screen, never recap a past one.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/04-CONTENT-AUTHORING.md` (188-189): “Fixed by rewriting all 16 `teacher_recap` strings to present-tense current-step coaching”
- `grade3-reading/_archive/handovers/HANDOVER-2026-09-24.md` (262): “persistent 'What did Scout do?' button; Step 4 unskippable;”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (349): “A persistent button, "What did Scout do?", reopens them at any point during Part 3, and a child can jump straight to one step rather than replaying the whole thing.”
- `g9/library/text/L8d5159.txt` (349): “This is separate from the "I need help" button, which calls the refresher: this one is a reference, not a rescue, and it costs the child nothing on the pacing signal.”

Against: A later run found: a repair in a Grade 3 reading course replaced carryover recaps of the worked example with summaries of the current task's own material, after a scan warned that an unsignposted recap about another character can be taken for a hint about the learner's own question (`grade3-reading/artifacts/qc-2026-09-29-staging/ADJUDICATION-pr662.md`). A later run found: A later ruling in a Grade 3 reading course bars hints that recap a past screen: a hint must coach the current screen, since one that re-narrates an earlier step or model action teaches the child to listen for the wrong screen, and all 16 recap strings were rewritten as present-tense coaching for the current step (`grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/04-CONTENT-AUTHORING.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/artifacts/qc-2026-09-29-staging/ADJUDICATION-pr662.md`).

### A reply that depends on the answer

Make every reply depend on what the learner actually said, have each hint add what the answer lacks, and test that every branch, including 'I don't know', can fire.

A research synthesis on the third turn of classroom talk, the reply after a child answers, notes that most instruction and most software stop at a confirmation such as "good", the weakest use of the moment (Shute 2008), and that the clearest sign of a real third turn is a next question that uses what the child just said (Nystrand et al. 1997). Its rule is that a reply that would be the same whatever the child said is not a real response and should be rebuilt. A built Grade 3 lesson applies the idea to choice questions: each wrong option is a coded misconception, and its feedback names that mismatch without quoting the key. A hint must add what the answer lacks, and every branch, including the one for 'I don't know', must be able to fire.

- `downloads/WF - Brainlift Opportunities to - 260724-110911.md` (71): “The clearest sign that the app has taken a real third turn is that its next question uses what the child just said (Nystrand et al., 1997). A reply that would read the same no matter what the child answered is not a third turn.”

Published: [Shute 2008](https://doi.org/10.3102/0034654307313795) · [Lyster & Saito 2010](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0272263109990520)

Against: A recast, the reply that restates the learner's idea more fully, often goes unnoticed by children and does not reliably teach one specific point, so where something must be learned exactly the same brainlift prefers asking the learner to say it again in their own words (Lyster & Saito 2010, https://doi.org/10.1017/S0272263109990520). A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/incept-read-app/docs/G4-SCR-example-native-qti.md`).

### A vocabulary floor

When the text supplies a stronger word than an answer uses, treat the answer as unfinished, not wrong: accept it, then push once for the text's word. In one design, a child who writes "nice" where the text says "kindest" is accepted and then asked once for the stronger word.

The closing-questions design requires at least one slot in its finding question to carry the text's own words, calling it the place where vocabulary is either held or lost (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-closing-questions-2026-09-24/Closing_Questions_Pattern.txt`). An answer in weaker words than the text supplies is unfinished, not wrong: it is accepted, then pushed once for the stronger word the text gives, and the rule is repeated as a floor for all three closing questions.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-closing-questions-2026-09-24/Closing_Questions_Pattern.txt` (82): “This is where vocabulary is either held or lost.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-closing-questions-2026-09-24/Closing_Questions_Pattern.txt` (83): “Do not accept an answer in weaker language than the text supplies.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-closing-questions-2026-09-24/Closing_Questions_Pattern.txt` (114): “A vocabulary floor. No answer accepted that uses weaker language than the text supplies, without one push first.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/handover/closing-questions/HANDOVER.md` (112-113): “on a phrase slot a one-word follow-up replaced the whole answer). The slot passes on meaning.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/knowledge-and-curriculum.md` (245-247): “Grade the feedback loop harder too: make the child revise until the answer uses precise vocabulary instead of passing a generic one.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/handover/closing-questions/HANDOVER.md` (111-113): “Vocabulary floor: the "find the stronger word" push was built and then REMOVED ... on a phrase slot a one-word follow-up replaced the whole answer). The slot passes on meaning.”

Against: A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/handover/closing-questions/HANDOVER.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/knowledge-and-curriculum.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/handover/closing-questions/HANDOVER.md`).

<sub>criterion · feedback, wording, marking · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Acknowledge where there is no right answer

Answer a prompt that has no right answer with an acknowledgement of what the learner said: nothing prewritten, which can give away what comes, and no correction or next step. When one build's suppression of prewritten replies was disabled on purpose, 5 of its gate's 10 checks went red, including the one for character names leaking.

A Grade 3 reading build opens by asking what the child already knows, before they have read a word, and its reply was assembled from writing prepared in advance that knew the story: it named characters and told the child what happens. A brief version that also showed the marker's suggested next step produced a panel headed 'One more thing' whose one more thing was a direction to start reading, a correction heading delivering a stage direction. The fix prints nothing written in advance on those beats, only an acknowledgement and what the child actually said, and stops there, since the beats have no right answer and so no next step. Its gate passes 10/10, and 5 of its 10 checks went red when the suppression was disabled on purpose.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/CASE-FOR-APPROVAL.md` (132-133): “it named characters and told the child what happens.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/CASE-FOR-APPROVAL.md` (133): “A warm-up meant to find out what a child brings was handing them the plot instead.”

### An invitation cue ends at first tap

Stop a cue inviting a first tap once the student has tapped that surface, and never restart it after a miss, where it reads as 'tap me again'.

A review of a Grade 3 reading course saw wrong clues blink again after a child tapped them and tapped outside, which read as an invitation to tap them again. The ruling makes the pulse an attract cue for before first engagement only: it switches off on each surface after the child's first tap there and never restarts on a miss or a change of state. A beat that arrives later on the same passage still gets its own one-time invitation on its own surface.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (148-149): “the wrong clues blink again after tapping them and tapping outside, which reads as 'tap me again'.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (141-142): “The attract pulse latches off per surface, not per passage, after the child's first tap on that surface, and never restarts on a miss or state swap”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (142-143): “the pulse is an attract cue for before first engagement only; blinking again after a wrong tap reads as "tap me again."”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans/courses/g3-reading/iteration-6/distilled/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (143-145): “A later-arriving beat on the same passage still earns its own one-shot invitation on ITS surface.”

### Announce each new verdict once

Announce each new piece of feedback once to assistive technology and keep restored earlier feedback quiet, so a screen-reader user never hears an old verdict as new. The repaired status passed 5 of 5 checks on the real components, each of which the old build failed as intended.

An audit of reading lessons found that several answer types, among them free responses, sequences and checked multi-select items, gave no lasting accessible update, while wrapping the whole reply history in a live region would have replayed old verdicts on every revisit. The repair put a small, stable, polite status at each interaction that announces each newly settled verdict, stays quiet for restored or repeated history and respects pending and outage states. Its tests passed 5 of 5 on the real components against 5 intended failures on the old build.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/bead-specs/announcements.md` (6): “Several Reply owners lack a persistent accessible update, while generic live-wrapping history would replay old verdicts.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/bead-specs/announcements.md` (8): “A small stable polite status at interaction owners announces each new settled identity, suppresses restored/repeated history and respects pending/outage transitions.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/bead-specs/announcements.md` (4): “Submit a free response, sequence or checked MSQ; inspect the stable live region and revisit/history transitions.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json` (3223): “realReact5/5candidate vs5intendedbaselinefailures”

Against: Untested with real assistive technology: "Actual assistive-tech speech remains separately open at reading-9ur.17." (grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json, line 3223)

### Answer a missed word with its sound

When a spoken word isn't accepted, show what was heard, the printed word and its say-it spelling, and offer, never force, its sound, since writing alone can't teach pronunciation.

In a grade 3 reading course a child's missed word once got the reply 'What I heard: Esip. Start by saying the word itself', which named neither the word nor its sound and sent the child back to repeat whatever they believed it sounded like. The rule now has the reply name what was heard, print the word and its say-it spelling and offer the guide saying it again, composed in code from the transcript and the word rather than by a model (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md`, lines 258-263). An earlier version replayed the clip before every retry and was dropped as one control too many: offering the sound is the part that teaches, and playing it over the child only slows the retry.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md` (236-239): “When a child says a word and the gate does not accept it, the reply names what was heard, prints the word and its say-it spelling, and offers the guide saying it again. The sound is always ON OFFER and never imposed: Try again opens the microphone straight away.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md` (241): “No amount of written feedback teaches a pronunciation.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md` (250): “Offering the sound is the part that teaches.”

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md` (242-244): “named neither the word nor its sound, and sent them straight back to the microphone to repeat whatever they already believed the word sounded like.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/audio-and-voiceover.md` (236-238): “the reply names what was heard, prints the word and its say-it spelling, and offers the guide saying it again.”

### Ask only for what can be done now

Tie every instruction, hint and status line to the step the learner is on, never an earlier or hidden one, and ask only for what they can do now. An independent visual review found 40 word-meaning cards whose status told students to explain the word before any box for explaining it was on screen.

An audit of a Grade 3 and a Grade 5 reading course found three requests the screen could not meet. A lesson's opening rubric required tapping a line before the tapping control existed, a rehearsal page asked for a written plan with no box and no place named for it, and an independent visual review caught 40 word-meaning cards telling students to explain the word before the explanation step appeared. The repairs made the rubric ask for what a spoken or typed answer can supply, named a notebook for the plan and gave each card the instruction for its current stage. Earlier tests had never asked whether every instruction to produce something had a place to do it, and the course's published rules say every question on screen has an input.

### Ask why before testing a bare stance

When a learner gives a position without a reason, ask why before arguing against it.

A 3D version's closing debate treats an answer of three words or fewer with no word such as 'because' as a bare stance, a position with no reason. On the first round it names the stance and asks one 'why' question, with a pointer to a moment that bears on the question, holding its counter-reading back until a reason comes. Its marking gate expects a bare 'no' to a checkpoint asking 'Is it real? How do you know?' to come back partial: a bare yes or no is marked partial when the question also asks why.

Quoted:

- `othello-archive/server/tasks/close.mjs` (41): “a bare stance ("no", "yes", "kind of") is a position with no reason: ask for the why before testing it”
- `othello-archive/server/tasks/close.mjs` (49): “Their position is a bare stance with no reason given. Do not argue against it yet.”
- `othello-archive/scripts/qc-judge-cases.json` (298): “"answer": "no",”

### Ask why the wrong choice is wrong

On a wrong choice, ask the learner why that option is not right before revealing the answer, so a retry becomes a thinking move instead of another guess. In one design the move takes 0:25 and fires only on a wrong answer.

A Grade 3 reading lesson design adds a diagnose-the-distractor task to its task bank from Unit 2: on a wrong answer, ask why that one was not right before revealing (`g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx`, lines 229-234). It rehearses no test item but turns the retry ladder into a thinking move, takes 0:25 and fires only on a wrong answer. The design's guardrails table proposes the same move for a child who collects the wrong evidence, as a match for tracking mistakes rather than grading (lines 74-77).

Quoted:

- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (230): “On a wrong answer, ask why that one was not right before revealing.”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (231): “None. Turns the retry ladder into a thinking move.”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (233): “Only fires on a wrong answer.”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (75): “Diagnose the distractor: why is that one not it, before revealing.”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (77): “Proposed. Matches tracking mistakes rather than grading.”

Against: In the guardrails table the move is still a proposal (`g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx`, line 77).

### Attempts, feedback and reveal as one design

Design and test an item's allowed attempts, feedback steps, marking criteria, reveal and service-failure exit together, so no attempt runs past the written feedback or strands the learner. Three spoken-answer turns had gates whose configured attempts, criteria, feedback stages and reveal could not complete the written escalation.

A case study of one Grade 3 reading lesson found three spoken turns whose configured attempts, rubric shape, criteria, feedback stages and reveal behaviour could not complete the authored escalation; each setting had been checked on its own, and no trace followed wrong, partial, empty and infrastructure-failure answers through every attempt (`grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Peter-Pan-Quality-Case-Study-2026-08-24.md`, lines 217-223). The repair gave the turns complete assessment contracts and distinct escalation paths, under the rule that retry count is pedagogy and that attempts, criteria, rubric, support, reveal and the service-failure exit form one behavioural contract, to be checked by live traces of correct, partial, misconception, silent, repeated-failure and unavailable-speech answers (same file, lines 225-233). The course rulebook lists configured attempts that exceed the authored feedback, and speech failures that trap the learner, among its failure signals, and makes retry or reveal behaviour beyond the authored feedback ladder a stop condition for any readiness claim (`grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Perfect-Course-Rulebook.md`, lines 387-388 and 535). It also keeps success after repair apart from first-try evidence, and allows an exit from an infrastructure failure only after the learner has had the authored support (same file, lines 391-393).

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Peter-Pan-Quality-Case-Study-2026-08-24.md` (219-220): “Three spoken turns had gates, but configured attempts, rubric shape, criteria, feedback stages, and reveal behavior could not complete the authored escalation.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Peter-Pan-Quality-Case-Study-2026-08-24.md` (222-223): “No trace followed wrong, partial, empty, and infrastructure-failure responses through every attempt.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Peter-Pan-Quality-Case-Study-2026-08-24.md` (228-229): “Retry count is pedagogy. Attempts, criteria, rubric, support, reveal, and service- failure exit form one behavioral contract.”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Perfect-Course-Rulebook.md` (388): “configured attempts exceed authored feedback; unsupported speech failure traps the learner;”
- `grade3-reading/ti-courseplans-wiki/Perfect-Course-Rulebook.md` (535): “configured retry/reveal behavior exceeds the authored feedback ladder;”

Against: none found

### Buttons tell the truth

A button tells the truth: it names what it does, never claims understanding or previews the next page, and when locked stays visible and says in words what unlocks it. One lesson's Next sat padlocked for six screens with no reason given and no cue that it would unlock.

A UI review of a Grade 3 reading lesson found a forward button labelled 'I know what to watch for', a claim the child cannot fail to make, so it asserted understanding without checking any, and a Next control padlocked for six screens with no reason given, which an eight-year-old reads as 'you are not allowed'. The course renamed the label to a plain 'Next page' and kept its reading and answer gates independent of it, and its repair shows the current action's status beside Back with a labelled held Next. Its QA checklist, under 'Buttons tell the truth', fails a locked forward control that vanishes or gives no reason, and its published rules bar a button that announces the next page's content.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (46): “is a claim the child cannot fail to make. They will tap it regardless, so it asserts understanding without checking any.”

### Code prompts for decoding, meaning prompts for comprehension

During word reading, prompt a stuck reader with the letters and sounds rather than 'what would make sense', and during comprehension, prompt for meaning and reasoning.

An opportunities-to-respond BrainLift for early reading comprehension, sent as the playbook for a Grade 3 reading course, keeps code-based prompts out of comprehension talk and meaning-based prompts out of decoding (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/pedagogy/otr-brainlift-2026-07-24.md`, line 104). For word reading it follows an IES practice guide on foundational reading skills for kindergarten to grade 3 (the same file, line 209). It is a rule taken from that guide; nothing here measured it in a course.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/pedagogy/otr-brainlift-2026-07-24.md` (104): “Keep code-based prompts out of comprehension talk and meaning-based prompts out of decoding.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/pedagogy/otr-brainlift-2026-07-24.md` (104): “During word reading, prompt with the code rather than asking “what would make sense””

Published: [Foorman et al. 2016, Foundational Skills to Support Reading for Understanding in Kindergarten Through 3rd Grade (NCEE 2016-4008)](https://ies.ed.gov/ncee/wwc/PracticeGuide/21)

Against: The BrainLift was written for PreK to Grade 2 (the same file, line 12), and the course that adopted it leaves decoding out from Grade 3 on (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/knowledge-and-curriculum.md`, lines 209-212), so the word-reading half applies only where decoding is still taught.

### Code-written lines say only what code knows

Lines that code writes around any text should say only what code can know, such as who speaks or which section comes next, never what the passage means.

The 3D export writes a guide box before and after each section of whatever text it is given. Its code names who speaks in the next section, read from the text's speaker marks, or asks the reader to read slowly with the question in mind, and its comment rules out anything more: who speaks, never the content. Code can read who speaks and where a section falls but cannot know what a passage means, so lines held to the first stay true for any text.

Quoted:

- `g9/transformer/src/glue/threejs-render.js` (114): “Plain lines of ours: who speaks, never the content.”
- `g9/transformer/src/glue/threejs-render.js` (118): “k === 0 ? "first section" : k === secs.length - 1 ? "final section" : "next section";”
- `g9/transformer/src/glue/threejs-render.js` (119): “return say(who ? `In this ${where}, ${who}.` : `In this ${where}, read slowly, with the question in mind.`);”
- `g9/transformer/src/glue/threejs-render.js` (109): “const speakers = (sec) => [...new Set(sec.blocks.filter((b) => b.k === "sp" && b.who).map((b) => title1(b.who)))];”

<sub>criterion · wording, integrity · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Controls never cover text or leave the screen

No control or pop-up may cover reading text or leave the screen at any width, even after a resize. After the window narrowed with a definition open, the pop-up sat at left 328.59 px and right 640.59 px in a 390 px viewport.

At phone and tablet widths a fixed view toggle sat over the end of a passage's first sentence and hid part of a word, with no tooltip open. A glossary definition opened at laptop width stayed at left 328.59 px, right 640.59 px in a 390 px viewport after the window narrowed, and page-level overflow checks did not catch it. Beside a long answer, an undo label collapsed into a narrow column 37.27px wide, and the expected fix reserves the control's space independently of the answer's length.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/README.md` (59): “The fixed view toggle sits over the end of the first sentence, hiding part of “sleep.””
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/README.md` (59): “Reading content should remain unobstructed by controls.”
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/staging-full-sweep/README.md` (44): “Its measured bounds are left **328.59 px**, right **640.59 px**, width **312 px** in a **390 px** viewport.”
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/staging-full-sweep/README.md` (45): “Expected: reposition the open definition inside the viewport or dismiss it when layout changes.”
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/laptop-student-sweep/L2-ordering-undo-wrap.md` (16): “Expected: a stable readable undo label, with space reserved independently of the answer length.”
- `grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/laptop-student-sweep/L2-ordering-undo-wrap.md` (24): “undo button is 37.27px wide × 63px high”

Against: The pop-up case holds only for a resize while it is open: "Scope: confirmed for a viewport change with the popover already open. Opening it fresh on mobile fits; reopening is a workaround." (grade3-reading/visual-findings-2026-09-07/staging-full-sweep/README.md, line 47)

<sub>criterion · accessibility, stimulus · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Correct briefly, retest later

Correct an error immediately and briefly, test it, give other items, then retest it later. One lesson-authoring skill keeps corrective feedback to 40 words or fewer, with the passage in view, followed by a correct-test-retest flow.

Among the direct-instruction and practice rules a course review applied as its standard: error correction should be immediate and brief (correct, test, give intervening items, then retest later), and too much feedback can reduce active processing, so feedback timing should shift as proficiency improves (`grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0086-round2-feedback-brainlifts-2026-07-09.qmd`). The reading project's unpromoted lesson-authoring skill writes corrective feedback of at most 40 words with the passage visible, and follows a miss with the correct-test-retest flow (`grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0123-teacher-g3-reading-candidate-skill.qmd`).

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0086-round2-feedback-brainlifts-2026-07-09.qmd` (143): “- Error correction should be immediate and brief: correct, test, give intervening items, then retest later.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0086-round2-feedback-brainlifts-2026-07-09.qmd` (161): “- Too much feedback can reduce active processing; feedback timing should shift as proficiency improves.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0123-teacher-g3-reading-candidate-skill.qmd` (45-46): “≤40-word passage-visible corrective feedback with the DI Correct-Test-Retest miss-flow”
- `g9/library/text/L8d5159.txt` (83): “Reveal, then say aloud and re-retrieve before moving on.”
- `g9/library/text/L8d5159.txt` (85): “House rule: two attempts then reveal. The say-aloud comes from the 24-hour gate spec.”
- `g9/sources/Readventure - Lesson Design and Scaffolding - Updated.docx` (83): “Reveal, then say aloud and re-retrieve before moving on.”

Against: Help before an attempt is a different matter: a later course review held that an immediately available hint lowers desirable difficulty, and the rule there is attempt first, then targeted help (`grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0172-g3-reading-course-feedback-2026-08-04.qmd`).

<sub>criterion · feedback, practice · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Correct what a lure might teach

Follow a plausible wrong option with corrective feedback or a correction after the test, because a lure left uncorrected can plant false knowledge.

The reading project's assessment rules require product-appropriate corrective feedback or post-test correction for potent misconception-based distractors, because plausible lures can seed false knowledge. The rule offers two ways to meet it: corrective feedback suited to the product, or a correction after the test. The rule matters most for a wrong option built on a real misconception, and students can later recall the planted false knowledge as true.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0164-g3-v2-feedback-agent-routing.qmd` (95-96): “post-test correction because plausible lures can seed false knowledge;”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0159-mcq-principles-blueprints.qmd` (53): “Distractor quality and feedback quality are coupled decisions — you cannot set one without the other”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0159-mcq-principles-blueprints.qmd` (54): “Competitive, misconception-based distractors are the gold standard for both diagnosis and deep retrieval — but they carry the highest risk of the negative suggestion effect, where exposure to plausible wrong answers implants false knowledge.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0159-mcq-principles-blueprints.qmd` (54): “feedback largely neutralises this risk, while the absence of feedback leaves it unchecked”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0160-multiple-choice-questions-evidence-base.qmd` (121): “Without feedback, the negative suggestion effect persists: students continue to produce MC lures on later tests.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0160-multiple-choice-questions-evidence-base.qmd` (122): “With feedback, the negative suggestion effect is substantially (though not completely) eliminated.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0164-g3-v2-feedback-agent-routing.qmd` (95-96): “potent misconception-based distractors require product-appropriate corrective feedback or post-test correction because plausible lures can seed false knowledge”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/handoffs/g3-reading-v2-feedback-2026-07-16/feedback-obligations.jsonl` (48): “Potent MCQ lures are corrected before they can become retained false knowledge.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0160-multiple-choice-questions-evidence-base.qmd` (123): “Lab setting. Feedback was simple (correct answer shown).”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/docs/handoffs-2026-07-13/course-eval-layer/labeled-defect-corpus.jsonl` (5): “Corrective feedback did not match the construct the distractor represents (feedback not misconception-keyed to the chosen option).”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/skills/course-red-team/references/finding-taxonomy.md` (166): “strong misconception distractors + no feedback = teaching the error (negative suggestion effect)”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/skills/g3-reading-red-team/references/dimensions/D5-assessment-item-construction.md` (17): “Every misconception distractor carries feedback addressing that misconception”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/skills/g3-reading-red-team/references/dimensions/D5-assessment-item-construction.md` (18): “Any item with competitive/plausible distractors must provide feedback (guards the negative-suggestion effect)”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/docs/standards/student-course-quality/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (768): “potent lures receive correction”
- `ti-courseplans-audit/docs/standards/student-course-quality/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (769): “false lure left uncorrected”

Published: [Feedback enhances the positive effects and reduces the negative effects of multiple-choice testing (Memory & Cognition, 2008)](https://doi.org/10.3758/MC.36.3.604)

Against: the study was run in a lab and its feedback simply showed the correct answer, so whether feedback that only says where to look does the same was not tested. The same course's theme checkpoint is feedback-free by design (`ti-courseplans-audit/docs/practice-layer-spec.md`), so a plausible lure there goes uncorrected; the rule holds where feedback is given.

<sub>criterion · feedback, distractors · universal · from g3, research · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-3-round-2](../research/2026-10-12-3-round-2/), [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/)</sub>

### Critique that states high standards

Open critical feedback on a learner's work with a fixed line saying the standard is high and they can reach it, then give specific critique, not praise padding. In randomized field trials, 71% of African American students revised an essay returned with such a note, against 17% with a neutral note.

The Grade 9 research brief read the wise-feedback trials in full: essays returned with a note stating very high expectations and confidence that the student could reach them were revised far more often, 71% against 17% with a neutral note among African American students (covariate-adjusted) and 87% against 62% among White students, and a third study, in a high school, raised African American students' grades. The brief makes it a rule for the lesson's AI marker: every critique opens with a fixed sentence stating high standards and confidence in the student, then gives specific, critical feedback, not praise padding such as 'Great try!' before a hint.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (23): “Among African American students, 71% revised with this note versus 17% with a neutral note (covariate-adjusted).”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (26): “Every critique the LLM rubric returns should open with a fixed sentence stating high standards and confidence in the student.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (26): “It should give specific, critical feedback, not praise padding.”

Published: [Yeager, Purdie-Vaughns, Garcia et al. 2014](https://www.columbia.edu/cu/psychology/vpvaughns/assets/pdfs/Yeager_Breaking_the_Cycle_2013.pdf)

Against: The trials were randomized field trials, mostly in grade 7, with one in a high school (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md`, lines 23 and 25); the fixed sentence has not been tried in critiques a model writes.

### Enrolment-safe references to earlier learning

Refer to earlier learning in words true whatever a learner was taught before, never as 'last year you learned', and let a course's first lesson assume nothing earlier.

A reading course's sign-off for its first lesson requires re-encounter phrasing, not 'last year you learned', and models it with 'In one well-known myth'; notes for the unit's film say a line such as 'you already learned Greek myths last year' breaks this, since nothing guarantees what a learner was taught before. A team ruling on the course's first lesson goes further: as the first thing a child ever sees, it may reference nothing earlier, no 'remember when', no 'last time', and no earlier text, character, tool or word treated as known. It is the wording side of assuming only what every path guarantees.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/video/u1-course-plan-notes.md` (281-283): “**Reactivate, do not re-teach, and stay enrollment-safe.** `U1L1-SIGNOFF.md` Part 1 requires re-encounter phrasing, "NOT 'last year you learned'", and models it with "In one well-known myth …". A video that says "you already learned Greek myths last year" breaks this.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/authoring/inputs/rule-packs/reading-g3.json` (32): “It may reference NOTHING earlier: no 'remember when', no 'last time', no earlier text, character, tool or word treated as known.”

Against: none found

### Every exchange needs a defined end

Give every open-ended task or exchange a defined end, such as a completed paragraph or a sign the idea is understood, so it doesn't run until someone gives up.

In the reviewed Grade 9 prototype, a learner's substantively wrong second answer in a quote break-down conversation was half-corrected and the conversation moved on; nothing told the child or the app when the quote had been understood (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, line 338). The review contrasts a stem-based task, which ends when the paragraph is complete, with a chat, which ends when someone gives up, and its build checklist asks for a convergence criterion so the break-down has a defined end point (lines 338 and 129). It does not say what that criterion should be.

Quoted:

- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (338): “and the Archivist half-corrects, then moves on. Nothing tells the child, or the app, when the quote has been understood. A stem-based task ends when the paragraph is complete; a chat ends when someone gives up.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (129): “Give the quote break-down a convergence criterion, so there is a defined end point.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (129): “Give the quote break-down a convergence criterion, so there is a defined end point.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (338): “Nothing tells the child, or the app, when the quote has been understood. A stem-based task ends when the paragraph is complete; a chat ends when someone gives up.”

Against: none found

### Every hint rung adds something

Each rung of a hint ladder must add information and lead toward what the marking asks for. The first hint on all 28 word cards in one course restated the task the student had just failed.

A review of the reading course found the first hint on all 28 word cards was the same boilerplate, a restatement of the say-back task the child had just failed, so the first rung added no information anywhere in the course. A later round's fix gave one card a second hint about what grass needs to grow, so a stuck child who reasoned correctly from it would talk about light rather than the word's meaning and fail the rubric. The course's draft gate standard now requires every open response to carry a first hint, a second hint that is not the first reworded, and a reveal: three scored turns, escalating, ending in the one line allowed to state the answer. A first hint that restates the task adds nothing, and one that points elsewhere fails the student who follows it.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/g5-course/ADVERSARIAL-REVIEW-2026-08-19-R2.md` (117): “a restatement of the say_back task the child just failed. Rung 1 adds zero”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/g5-course/ADVERSARIAL-REVIEW-2026-08-20-R7.md` (119): “correctly from the hint given ("light") will say something about light, not about a home or”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/team-direction-2026-09-09/GATE-STANDARD-DRAFT.md` (54): “first reworded, and a `reveal` — three scored turns, escalating, ending in the one line allowed”

A threshold there would fail good content.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (491-493): “It fails a criterion with no `second_hint`, a `second_hint` identical to its `hint`, an assessment with no `reveal`, and any of them too short to say anything.”

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (410-412): “**Second miss: a closer hint.** The same missing piece, said more concretely: where in the text to look, or the shape of a sentence that would answer it. It may not be the first hint reworded.”

<sub>criterion · hints, scoring-guide · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Every instruction legible at 4.5:1

Set every line that tells a learner what to do, hints and help included, at a contrast of at least 4.5:1, in sentence case and upright type. An active help control measured 2.52167:1 and a textarea placeholder about 3.15:1, below the 4.5:1 normal text needs.

A supplied UI review found the labels that tell a child what to do set in small gold, some in all capitals and one in italics, and called them the hardest lines on the screen to read (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md`, lines 20 and 52). Computed styles then measured an active help control at 2.52167:1 and a textarea placeholder at approximately 3.15:1, where normal-size instructional text needs 4.5:1 under the course's design contract and WCAG 1.4.3 (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json`, lines 1769 and 1551). The repairs set the labels in ink and their natural case at weight 600, with the smallest raised to 14px, and the placeholder later measured 7.111:1 (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md`, line 13; `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json`, line 1563).

- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (52): “Instructional copy gets the least legible treatment available”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json` (1769): “Native computed style gives15px faint text on paper (2.52167:1) with57.96875x24.5px box.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json` (1551): “The placeholder composites to approximately 3.15:1 on white.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json` (1551): “This is normal-size instructional text, requiring 4.5:1 under the existing design contract and WCAG 1.4.3.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md` (10): “use ink for labels, sentence case and display sans for instruction.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md` (13): “Existing ink, natural authored case, normal tracking, weight600; the three13px-or-smaller instructional labels use14px and1.45 leading.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md` (19): “upright instructional sans note.”

Against: The 4.5:1 bar is WCAG's for normal-size text, and large text needs 3:1: "WCAG AA asks for 4.5:1 for body text and 3:1 for large text, so they fail both." (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md`, line 20).

<sub>criterion · accessibility, wording · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Every line true to the source

Everything a lesson says, quoted or not, from model answers and feedback to marking guides, must be true to its source and claim no more than the source's words support. One marking guide failed a student who gave the source's four pairings and passed one who gave the wrong pairings it named.

A source-fidelity review of the reading course found a model answer that chained two separate consequences into a mechanism the source never states, the same merged mechanism in corrective feedback, feedback that half-conceded a wrong option's claim that an action always works, and a marking guide that failed a student giving the source's four pairings while passing the wrong ones it named. A check of another lesson found five feedback and reveal lines that contradicted the story. The project's protocol for a text's claims about the world holds any text with a failed claim as a draft, since a wrong claim in a child's science text is a kill, not a footnote. What a lesson tells a student includes its model answers and feedback as well as its hints and definitions.

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/g5-course/ADVERSARIAL-REVIEW-2026-08-19-R3.md` (88): “criterion; a child stating the authored wrong pairs passes. The node's own grounding”

<sub>criterion · integrity, feedback, scoring-guide, stimulus · universal · from g3, g9 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Every turn of a hint ladder stays visible

Through a hint sequence, keep every earlier answer and hint on screen, since a hint that builds on the last answer is useless once that answer is gone.

A reading course runs a typed or spoken open answer as a conversation of three scored turns, a hint, a closer hint and then the answer, and its rule keeps every turn on screen, so the child can see what they already said and what they were already told (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md`, lines 403-420). A hint written to build on the last answer works only while that answer is still there to read.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (418-420): “Every turn stays on screen. The child can see what they already said and what they were already told, because a hint that builds on their last answer is useless beside an empty box where that answer used to be.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (405-406): “**Rule.** A typed or spoken open response is a conversation with three scored turns, and the reply escalates on each one.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (418-420): “Every turn stays on screen. The child can see what they already said and what they were already told, because a hint that builds on their last answer is useless beside an empty box where that answer used to be.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (423): “A criterion met on any turn stays met for the rest of the question.”

Against: none found

<sub>criterion · hints, feedback · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Every way out shows the whole answer

Show the complete authored answer on every way out of an open question, including a pass, the last miss and a scoring outage, since passing answers are often partial. After two scoring failures, the default limit, the authored answer appears and the learner may move on, with the attempt left unscored.

A younger-grade renderer gives an open response three scored turns, a hint, a closer hint and a reveal that gives the answer, and ends a pass the same way, with the praise and the whole answer in one reply, because a passing answer is usually half a sentence and its writer would otherwise never read the complete version. A later sign-off ruling on writing frames shows the one correct answer in the blank when the turns run out or the answer is right. The renderer also found that four surfaces that could strand a child each offered only a Move on button after repeated scoring failures, the one exit through which a child left a question knowing less than it asked, at a moment they had done nothing wrong, so that exit now shows the authored reveal first. A later scoring audit records an unavailable scorer's attempt as unscored, spending no try, banking no criterion and never passing, and shows the authored answer after the failure limit, two by default, before Move on.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (415-416): “**Third miss: the reveal.** Stop hinting. Name the gap, give the answer, say where in the text it came from, and open the gate.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (433): “**A pass ends the same way a reveal does: with the whole answer, in one reply.**”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (434-435): “A passing answer is usually half a sentence, and the child who wrote it is the one child who never gets to read the complete version.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/process/CONTRACT-SIGNOFFS.md` (253-254): “That answer is shown in the blank after all of the turns are exhausted, or when the student gets the answer correct.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (539-540): “**Rule.** Where a gate lets a child past after repeated infrastructure failures, it shows the authored `reveal` before it lets them go.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (544): “Four surfaces could strand a child”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (545-547): “each offered the same escape after `gate.max_tries` consecutive failures: a button reading "Move on", and nothing else.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (547-549): “That is the only exit in the app through which a child leaves a question knowing less than it asked of them, and it fires at the one moment they have done nothing wrong.”

### Explain the course once

Explain how a course works once, at first open, with a help entry to return to it, instead of re-explaining the machinery at the start of every unit.

The review of a Grade 9 prototype found each unit's opening doing two jobs, re-explaining how the program works and introducing the text, made explaining the program once, at first open, a P1 build item, and asked for a help entry as a way back for a student who joins mid-year or returns after a gap (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, lines 57-58). Its note argues that every second spent re-explaining the machinery from unit two onward is a second not spent on the text, and a recurring source of the sense that the lesson is long (line 140).

Quoted:

- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (57): “Explain the program once, at first open. Remove the per-unit re-explanation of how the program works.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (58): “Provide a way back to that explanation for a student who joins mid-year or returns after a gap — a help entry, not a repeated beat.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (140): “Every second spent re-explaining the machinery in unit two onward is a second not spent on the text, and it removes a recurring source of the sensation that the lesson is long — the opening of each unit currently has to do two jobs.”

Against: The reason given, that re-explaining the machinery takes time from the text and makes the lesson feel long, is a review note, which the review offers as rationale that has not been confirmed (`g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt`, line 13).

### Explanations must fit the item given

Explanations, hints and feedback must be true of the item as given. A judge scored factual accuracy 1.0 on an item whose explanation wrongly called a wrong option a fact stated explicitly in the text.

In one reported case the judge scored factual accuracy 1.0 on an inference item whose explanation called a wrong option 'a fact stated explicitly in the text' when that claim was not in the passage, because it accepted the explanation's description of itself without checking (`icloud-work/inceptbench-main/tests/test_evaluator_prompt_rules_967.py`). The reading and writing rules now make the judge verify such labels against the passage, and the ELA rules forbid using the explanation as the sole source of truth (`icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/sat_rw_evaluation.txt`; `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/ela_evaluation.txt`). The closed-ended rubric treats explanations, hints, feedback templates and diagnostic notes as part of the content and fails an item whose support text discusses values that are not among its options, counting it under clarity rather than accuracy when code has confirmed the answer (`icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/closed_ended_evaluation.txt`). An explanation is written for the options' final order and backs the keyed option and no other, which code checks, failing the item when it can't tell. A judge checks every other claim that explanations, hints and feedback make against the item itself.

Quoted:

- `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/tests/test_evaluator_prompt_rules_967.py` (10): “The evaluator accepted the explanation's self-characterization without checking.”
- `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/sat_rw_evaluation.txt` (34-36): “**Rule:** When an explanation claims a distractor is "a fact stated explicitly in the text" or "explicitly stated," you MUST verify whether the *specific claim made in that distractor* is literally present in the stimulus.”
- `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/ela_evaluation.txt` (83): “Do NOT use `answer_explanation` as your sole source of truth — the explanation may be absent, wrong, or misleading.”
- `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/closed_ended_evaluation.txt` (260-261): “- **FIELD MISMATCH**: Explanations, hints, feedback templates, or `additional_details` describe distractors, answers, or values that do NOT match the actual options or correct answer - Example: `additional_details` discusses choosing between "7 riyals" and "14 riyals" but actual options are 28, 70, 30, 35”
- `icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/closed_ended_evaluation.txt` (271): “**CRITICAL - Supporting Text Fields**: You MUST treat explanations, hints, feedback templates, and diagnostic notes (`additional_details` fields) as part of the content.”

A pattern check catches only explicit letter phrases: an explanation can argue for a wrong option without naming it (`icloud-work/ap-social-studies-generator/python/benchmark_runs/analysis_iter1.md`).

### Feedback for each wrong option

Give each wrong option its own feedback that names the error and likely misconception it stands for and says what to look at next. In one course draft, none of the 1,234 option-specific feedback routes fell back to a generic message keyed to the option's position.

A review of one grade 5 course draft was closed with all 1,234 option-specific feedback routes having zero generic option-position IDs or truncated claim and recheck templates. The reading project's evaluator gap list asks for misconception-coded distractors and misconception-named feedback, and its course checklist requires every scored item to carry correct and incorrect feedback, with each wrong option mapped to a named misconception. One message for every wrong answer, generic encouragement or feedback keyed to an option's position is a defect.

Against: The same rubric requires feedback to state the correct answer or clearly guide the student to it (`icloud-work/inceptbench/submodules/reading-question-qc/qc_pipeline/config/prompts.json`, L77), which conflicts with feedback that sends the student back. The record shows the gate's result, not a case where feedback keyed to a position reached a student.

<sub>criterion · feedback, distractors · universal · from ap, g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Feedback must not look like a button

Make feedback and a guide's speech look unlike controls, keeping button styles for what acts when pressed, since learners may try to tap anything drawn like a button. One lesson used four button styles with no rule tying them to meaning.

A supplied UI review of a Grade 3 lesson found the guide's speech bubble drawn as a green outlined rounded rectangle, the same look as the lesson's pill buttons, and four button styles in one lesson with no rule connecting them to meaning (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md`, lines 28 and 33). The project kept the avatar and readable text but drew replies with a thin verdict rule, square corners and a transparent ground, keeping the action pill for deliberate click controls (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md`, line 18; `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md`, line 22).

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (33): “On `/p3` it is a green outlined rounded rectangle, the same visual language as the "Sound it out" and "The word parts" pills.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (33): “A child will try to tap it.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (28): “There are four button styles in one lesson and no rule connecting them to meaning.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md` (18): “Reply should retain avatar and readable text with a subtle rule/verdict marker; action pill belongs only to deliberate click controls.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md` (22): “Reply uses a1px bottom verdict rule, square corners, transparent background and500 text.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/qa/new-lesson-qa-invariants.md` (142-143): “**E3. Feedback is honest about affordance**: success feedback must never look like another tappable element (doc 1 item 7).”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/qa/new-lesson-qa-invariants.md` (143-144): “the verdict element has no role=button/cursor-pointer; OWED as a general sweep.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/DESIGN.md` (84): “Use borders, spacing and typography to separate feedback and controls.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/DESIGN.md` (84): “spoken feedback must not look like another button.”

Against: The tap prediction is the review's judgment, not observed with children, and the restyle is a source candidate, "not a deployed or final browser-accepted state" (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/STYLE-IMPLEMENTATION-by-residual.md`, line 3); the mapping marks it "Pending visual refinement" (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md`, line 18).

<sub>criterion · feedback, accessibility · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Feedback that sends the student back

Feedback on a wrong answer says where to look, never what the student has to produce, and only what is true of what they were given.

Code holds feedback that names the right answer or speaks of how a film is made ("the second frame"); the judge then
checks each line against what the film has said and shown by its stop, and against the text
(`g9/transformer/src/mechanics/util.js`, `stopChecks` and `feedbackJudged`). The checks exist because a finished block
had a line saying the film had said something it hadn't. Since then, drafts have been held for "the second frame" and
for feedback that named the answer, and the film check made again under these checks had all 9 lines confirmed
(`g9/transformer/v02/QA.md`, "Video essay and the unit-video modules"). A hint may point to the line to read, even
quote it, but copies no run of the answer (`g9/transformer/src/mechanics/open-check.js`); where the line itself is the
answer, the hint names who speaks or what moment to look at, without quoting it
(`g9/transformer/src/mechanics/evidence-hunt.js`); a nearly exact quotation is told it is near, without the text's
wording (`g9/transformer/src/glue/animated-judge.js`). The film kit's rule is the same: feedback on a wrong option
says why it is wrong and points back to the scene without naming the right one
(`othello-unit-video/.claude/skills/uvk-checks-make/SKILL.md`).
