### Feedback where the student is looking

Show feedback directly under the answer or option it responds to, and keep the text in view whenever feedback says to look back.

The reading course's checklist requires the reply to a wrong answer to render directly under the tapped option, checked by a code tripwire, and the passage to be on screen whenever feedback says to look back, as a blocking row (`grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md`, lines 72 and 103). A visual pass on one lesson found a correct reply rendered under a box on the right while the tapped line sat on the left, usually off screen (`grade3-reading/artifacts/peter-pan-standing-2026-09-11/VISUAL-QA-2026-09-11.md`, lines 43-46).

### Fit the hint to the kind of miss

Fit a recall hint to the kind of miss, naming a near spelling or another taught word rather than just saying wrong, and give the word on the second miss. A typed word within an edit distance of two is told it is nearly right instead of being given a letter.

The lesson engine's key-word deck copies a 3D version's gap rules in code: a typed word within an edit distance of two of the answer is told it is nearly right, a word from the same deck is shown its own meaning beside the cued one, anything else gets the answer's first letter, and the second miss gives the word with its meaning (`g9/transformer/src/mechanics/key-words.js`). A test plants a near spelling and a mix-up within the deck and checks each line (`g9/transformer/test/mechanics.test.mjs`).

Quoted:

- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/key-words.js` (119): “const line = dist(plain(w.word), n) <= 2 ? "Nearly. That is the word, but the spelling is not right yet."”

- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/key-words.js` (118): “Here is the word: "${w.word}" (${w.meaning}). Hold on to it: you will use it today.”

Against: The last hint gives away part of the answer: `The word you want begins with "${w.word.charAt(0).toUpperCase()}".` (`g9/transformer/src/mechanics/key-words.js`, line 121). Nothing measures whether these hints help recall. Whether the slip counts differs by task: in say it back "a near spelling of a long word is named but not counted as a miss" (g9/transformer/src/mechanics/say-it-back.js line 3), while in key words a near spelling "gets a nudge, since a taught word is spelled right" (g9/transformer/src/mechanics/key-words.js line 4) and comes back marked wrong (line 122).

### Frame skills as resisting manipulation

For adolescents, pitch a reading skill as a way to resist being manipulated, catching how a text works on the reader, rather than as practice of a skill. In a double-blind trial with 536 eighth graders, healthy eating framed as standing up to food-industry manipulation beat standard health education.

The Grade 9 research brief reports a double-blind randomized trial with 536 eighth graders in which framing healthy eating as standing up to food-industry manipulation beat standard health education, and students made healthier choices a day later in an unrelated setting. It rates this as one strong randomized trial, in health rather than reading, and draws the pitch for close reading: catch how the text works on you, not 'let's practice our skill'. The Grade 9 strategy adopts that pitch.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (18): “In a double-blind randomized trial with 536 eighth graders, framing healthy eating as standing up to food-industry manipulation beat standard health education.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (21): “Do not pitch it as "let's practice our skill."”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/strategy/grade-9-strategy.md` (49): “Close reading can be pitched the same way: catch how the text is working on you.”

Published: [Bryan, Yeager, Hinojosa et al. 2016](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/5047199)

Against: One trial, in healthy eating rather than reading (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md`, line 20); whether the framing helps reading is untested.

### Help after a try, cues first

Offer help only after the student has tried, and order hints from general cues to specific ones, naming the clue's own words only in the last hint.

A course feedback meeting warned that an immediately available hint lowers desirable difficulty and set the rule: attempt first, then targeted help. A census of the lessons flagged guided-practice nudges that named the clue's own words instead of a criterion cue, and the fix pattern rewords them as criterion cues, naming the clue's actual words only in the last nudge. The general cues say what to look for.

<sub>criterion · hints, feedback · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Help may paraphrase, not interpret

Help may paraphrase a hard line, say who speaks to whom or ask planned questions that draw out reasoning, but the interpretation, evidence and answer stay the learner's. The help for a tapped line may ask one question that makes the learner look at one word, but may not say what the line reveals about the character.

The line-help task in a 3D version of the Grade 9 lessons tells its model to help learners understand the line themselves: it may put a hard word into plain modern English, say who is speaking to whom and what has just happened, or ask one question that makes them look at one word, but must not interpret what the line reveals about the character or answer the learning question, in under 50 words (`othello-archive/server/api.mjs`, line 119). The boundary lives only in that prompt.

Quoted:

- `othello-archive/server/api.mjs` (119): “Help them understand the line themselves. You may: put a hard word into plain modern English, say who is speaking to whom and what has just happened, or ask one question that makes them look at one word.”
- `othello-archive/server/api.mjs` (119): “You must not: interpret what the line reveals about the character for them, or answer the learning question.”
- `g9/transcript/chat-transcript.redacted.txt` (4279): “We should distinguish questions that elicit the student’s reasoning from hints that supply it.”
- `g9/transcript/chat-transcript.redacted.txt` (3108): “Agents do not choose evidence, suggest interpretations or revise the response.”
- `g9/transcript/chat-transcript.redacted.txt` (4337): “The Archivist can ask the planned questions and listen, but the student should supply the interpretation, evidence and explanation.”

Against: The boundary was never tried with learners: per-line help was taken out of the reading (`othello-archive/src/steps/Reading.tsx`, line 3), and the client function for this task is defined but no longer called (`othello-archive/src/api.ts`, line 25). Untested with students, and how much help independent practice allows was left open: 'We should agree whether this means writing without co-authorship but with bounded hints, or demonstrating unaided understanding.' (`g9/transcript/chat-transcript.redacted.txt`, line 4392).

<sub>criterion · hints, demand · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Help that waits to be asked

Keep help quiet and optional: glosses, notes and a few named helpers open only when the learner asks or with feedback, never as a constant guide or per-part buttons.

Early drafts of a Grade 9 unit opening with a visible guide character and constant assistant behaviour were rejected, and the 6 October handover records that the required direction became a mature storytelling experience with quiet on-demand help (line 500); one of its design rules makes support quiet, optional and on demand (line 76). The rebuilt prototype offers three optional scripted helpers, one for a word's meaning or source context, one for a generic next step and one for a manageable way to begin, and during an independent attempt the meanings helper must not write the learner's claim or explain the interpretation being assessed (handover line 155; `g9/prototypes/review-build/DESIGN.md`, line 7).

Quoted:

- `g9-archive/handover-2026-10-06/Othello-handover-docs-and-transcript-2026-10-06.zip` (Othello-handover/Othello-project-handover.txt inside it) (500): “The visible Archivist concept and constant assistant behavior were not the desired approach. (line 2871), both in `g9/transcript/chat-transcript.redacted.txt`. The changes to the built lesson were proposed, not built or tried with students. The overload was a judgment, not a measurement, and no script checks the notes against the checkpoints, so the rule that a note never answers one is stated, not tested.

### Hints advance only on a changed answer

Advance a hint ladder only when the answer has changed since the last try, so resubmitting the same answer cannot unlock more help. One inference design asks an unchanged answer to try again and reveals the full words only on the third failed try.

A Grade 3 inference design gives its typed sentence frame a feedback ladder that advances only if the answer has changed since the last try. An empty or unchanged answer is only asked to have another go, and the full words are revealed on the third fail.

Quoted:

- `downloads/5. Curriculum/Instruction/3.1 - Fantasy Classics - Lesson Designs/3.1 - 2. Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp, Part 2 (FICTION, INFER) — v3 (new INFER design).docx` (311 (textutil text)): “FEEDBACK LADDER (only advances if the answer changed since the last try):”

- `downloads/5. Curriculum/Instruction/3.1 - Fantasy Classics - Lesson Designs/3.1 - 2. Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp, Part 2 (FICTION, INFER) — v3 (new INFER design).docx` (316 (textutil text)): “3rd fail: reveal the full words.”

### Hold feedback until the linked answer

When one answer leads straight into a linked one, hold feedback on the first until the second is given, because a verdict on the first can answer the second.

On the opening question of a Grade 3 reading build's main conversation, the child says an answer and then taps the line that proves it, and once spoken answers were read by a marker, a note about what the child said appeared under the reply to the tap. The note is held until after the tap on purpose, because a reply that reacts to whether the spoken answer was right would answer the tap before the child makes it.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/FEEDBACK-RESPONSES.md` (149): “on the opening question of the main conversation”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/FEEDBACK-RESPONSES.md` (151-152): “the child says their answer, taps the line that proves it, and gets a note about what they said underneath the reply to the tap.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/FEEDBACK-RESPONSES.md` (152-153): “We held the note until after the tap on purpose: a reply that reacts to whether the spoken answer was right would answer the tap before the child makes it.”

Against: The same build's two-part evidence items settle the first part before the second: "Part B is hidden until Part A is answered correctly" (`grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/CONTENT-SCHEMA.md`, line 270), so a learner knows the first part's verdict before the second part appears.

### Instructions a literal reader can follow

Write instructions a literal reader can follow: verbs match actions, units match what can be selected, and each fill-in frame stays grammatical and sensible for every possible value.

A review of a Grade 5 course found a prompt that kept a selection verb on a free-writing box, so a student would look for four sentences to pick, and an item that said to tap two sentences when each tappable unit was a whole paragraph (`grade3-reading/artifacts/g5-course/ADVERSARIAL-REVIEW-2026-08-19-R2.md`). An outside review of a Grade 3 lesson found a fill-in frame that broke grammar ('has a enchanting'); it was rewritten so it stays grammatical whichever word fills the blank (`grade3-reading/artifacts/baseline-submission/CHANGES-FOR-REVIEW-L4-2026-08-31.md`).

The shorthand "a(n) _____" is not an error in itself: it should resolve to the right article (`icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/closed_ended_evaluation.txt`, line 250).

<sub>criterion · wording, stem, format · universal · from ap, g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Keep adult controls off the learner's screen

Keep what is meant for adults, such as timers, build strings, counters and skip or review switches, off the learner's screen.

The reading project's QC checklist makes it a blocking rule that timers, build strings, counters, scripts and skip controls appear only in the preview mode, and its attention standard keeps them to the preview or the end-of-lesson receipt (`grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md`; `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/process/ATTENTION-HIERARCHY.md`). The checklist flagged a switch to the review view in the student page, warning that if it stayed every gate would be one tap from skippable, and a UI review agent later found that unlabelled switch on staging and on production: one tap put a child in the review view, where every gate opens (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-argus-ui-2026-09-30/REGISTER.md`). The fix renders the switch only once a page is already in review mode or a staging flag turns it on (the same register).

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md` (104): “Timers, build strings, counters, scripts, skip controls in previewer (?review=1) only; student DOM greps clean”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/process/ATTENTION-HIERARCHY.md` (44-46): “**Metadata lives with adults.** Grade/build strings, timers, page counters, scripts, skip controls: previewer (`?review=1`) or the receipt only.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md` (181): “if it stays, every gate is one tap from skippable”

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-argus-ui-2026-09-30/REGISTER.md` (11): “toggle renders only after `?review=1` (mode already review)”

<sub>criterion · integrity, practice · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Land the key idea for every learner

Put every part of a lesson's key idea where every learner meets it, such as the text, the narration or the closing screen, not only in replies to strong answers. A search of one lesson for its scripted key-idea sentence found 0 hits, and 2 of its 3 closing branches dropped the idea.

An audit searched one reading lesson for the key-idea sentence its design scripts and found zero hits: only the closing dialogue's branch for a learner who answered the hardest follow-up first time paraphrased it, and the partial and off branches, the ones struggling readers see, closed on other points. In a second lesson the key idea had two parts and the closing screen landed one, though the learner had just worked on the other. Re-verification confirmed both and proposed landing the idea on a surface every learner meets, the way another lesson states its key idea in the passage itself.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L6-2026-09-05.md` (138-140): “the doc's threshold sentence never lands verbatim, and two of three closing branches (the ones struggling readers see) drop the power-changes-hands frame entirely.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L6-2026-09-05.md` (148-149): “Grep of the full L6 JSON for "change hands and change people" returns **zero hits**.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L6-2026-09-05.md` (164-167): “So a child who does not land the hardest follow-up on the first try — i.e., exactly the reader this instructional design exists to serve — closes the lesson having heard only "Peter lost things," never "power changed hands," let alone the doc's fixed threshold sentence.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L6-2026-09-05.md` (177-181): “L10's construction makes verbatim landing performance-independent because the passage itself carries it — L6's threshold is an abstraction the KB layers onto the story (Hook never says "power has changed hands"), so it depends entirely on the wrap material to land it, and that wrap material only lands it, partially, for the top-branch child.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L4-2026-09-05.md` (145-149): “the KB threshold is a two-part compound ("rules AND mood"), and the closing screen only lands one part. A reviewer applying that rule literally would flag this: the child does the rules-inference work (p22 extra-thinking) and then the wrap never confirms or echoes it, so the second half of the day's threshold is not certified as landed.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-PEDAGOGY-2026-09-05.md` (15): “land the threshold in a universal surface (narration/receipt) the way L10 lands "brains beat strength"”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PEDAGOGY-L7-2026-09-05.md` (132-133): “the established lesson pattern (confirmed across L4 and L10) is paraphrase-not-verbatim”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0231-pp-depth-6h-doc-diffs.qmd` (52-53): “L5 and L6 each land their threshold sentence word-for-word somewhere; L7 does not.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-PEDAGOGY-2026-09-05.md` (14): “L4 is the reviewer-SIGNED baseline; fixing touches the signed lesson (no-churn).”

Against: The course accepts paraphrase rather than verbatim landing: "the established lesson pattern (confirmed across L4 and L10) is paraphrase-not-verbatim" (PEDAGOGY-L7-2026-09-05.md lines 132-133). An earlier pass conflicts with the zero-hit search: "L5 and L6 each land their threshold sentence word-for-word somewhere; L7 does not." (kb-0231-pp-depth-6h-doc-diffs.qmd lines 52-53). The fix for the two-part case was parked because that lesson is the signed baseline (REGISTER-PEDAGOGY-2026-09-05.md line 14).

<sub>criterion · feedback, evidence-of-learning, sequence · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Lines that support autonomy

Write what a lesson's guide says to listen, offer hints, encourage and acknowledge the learner's view, and leave out directives, 'you should' statements and handed-over answers. In a lab study, eight behaviours, offering hints among them, predicted higher perceived autonomy, and six, directives among them, predicted lower.

The Grade 9 research brief read in full a lab study of one-on-one sessions: listening, letting the student work their own way, student talk time, informational praise, encouragement, offering hints, answering the student's questions and acknowledging their perspective predicted higher perceived autonomy, while holding onto the materials, showing or saying the answers, directives, 'should / got to' statements and controlling questions predicted lower. The brief uses it as a style guide for the guide's lines and the model's prompts and bans 'you need to / you should / tap here', and the Grade 9 strategy adopts it as its line-writing guide.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (39): “Eight behaviors predicted higher perceived autonomy: listening, letting the student work their own way, time the student spends talking, informational praise, encouragement, offering hints, answering student questions, and acknowledging the student's perspective.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (39): “Six predicted lower autonomy: holding onto the materials, showing or saying the answers, directives, "should / got to" statements, and controlling questions.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (43): “Ban "you need to / you should / tap here."”

Published: [Reeve & Jang 2006](https://selfdeterminationtheory.org/SDT/documents/2006_ReeveJang_JEP.pdf)

Against: The study was correlational, in a lab, with adult participants in a 10-minute one-on-one session (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md`, line 41). Its supportive list includes informational praise, which the lesson engine's plain-voice rule bans from every line a student reads.

<sub>criterion · wording, hints, feedback · universal · from g9, research · external-research · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Mark independent writing live

While learners write on their own, mark their writing live in small, frequent touches, such as a word to swap, so they improve the text as they go.

A planning meeting for a Grade 9 course proposed live marking during independent practice as an effective secondary-school practice: learners write on their own while live marking moments suggest a word to swap, because, as the meeting put it, feedback is most effective little and often and in real time. The course's lesson specification then gave the guide the job of building sentence stems and giving live feedback that moves the learner toward the answer one step at a time. The meeting cited no study for the claim.

Quoted:

- `g9/sources/meeting-notes/ELA - CNU + Academics - 2026_10_01 20_30 IST - Notes by Gemini.md` (258): “So, one of the like a really effective practice um particularly in secondary schools is where you get kids just on their own writing, but there are like live marking moments where you know this word or swap out this word and they can be like making notes to just improve that text in real time because feedback is most effective when it's little and often um and in real time.”
- `g9/sources/meeting-notes/ELA - CNU + Academics - 2026_10_01 20_30 IST - Notes by Gemini.md` (23): “Explored incorporating live marking during independent practice to deliver real-time feedback.”
- `g9/sources/Year 9 ELA_ Lesson Design Specification for Devfactory.md` (182): “Build sentence stems and give live feedback that move the student toward the answer, one step at a time.”

Against: The same specification says the guide will never "Appear constantly or interrupt unprompted." (`g9/sources/Year 9 ELA_ Lesson Design Specification for Devfactory.md`, line 181), so live feedback has to come without unprompted interruption.

<sub>criterion · feedback, marking, practice · domain · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Modelled writing must be error-free

Text that models what students should write must be free of errors, because students may copy it. One lesson's modelled method lines had comma splices on three pages.

A review of one Grade 3 lesson found comma splices in the annotation lines that model the method on three pages and asked for them to be fixed, because those lines model the method for the child, so the punctuation is copied (`shadow/content-eval/reports/G3-U2-L1-review.md`). That students copy it is the review's reasoning, not a measurement.

Quoted:

- `shadow-content-eval/reports/G3-U2-L1-review.md` (208): “These lines model the method for the child, so the punctuation is copied.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/BEAD-REGISTER.json` (3020): “Inspect G3-U1-L6/p19 retell supplied example: it places ship march before Peter escapes, contrary to p10 and p11.”
- `g9/sources/Othello_1_Feedback_DevFactory.md` (300): “modeled writing is copy, and must be punctuated as publishable prose, because it is the only model of correct writing the child sees.”
- `g9/library/text/L36b8ba.txt` (300): “This is the second punctuation correction on modeled text, after Scout’s modeled answer on Aladdin, which makes it worth a standing rule: modeled writing is copy, and must be punctuated as publishable prose, because it is the only model of correct writing the child sees.”

Against: none found

### Name an option by its words

Never refer to an option by its letter or position in feedback, hints, explanations or instructions; name it by what it says, since the display may reorder options.

A reading course's lesson-structure rules note that the player re-letters options into display order, so feedback must never say 'option C'. Its invariants for new lessons, distilled from three feedback documents on five lessons, bar meta-language anywhere a child reads, such as 'blank two', 'option A' or 'the passage states', and name a sweep that checks for it.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/02-LESSON-STRUCTURE.md` (240-241): “The player RE-LETTERS options into display order — never write feedback that says "option C".”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/qc-2026-09-29-staging/new-lesson-qa-invariants.md` (146-147): “no meta-language anywhere child-facing ("blank two", "option A", "the passage states").”
- `grade3-reading/incept-read-app/NOTE-dose-teach-sections.md` (88-89): “**Answer positions are already balanced** across A/B/C/D and explanations never name a letter, so options are safe to shuffle on render if you want.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/CONTENT-SCHEMA.md` (192-193): “the first option shown is always "A" regardless of its authored key”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/CONTENT-SCHEMA.md` (194-195): “So the authored keys are identity tags, not positions — never write feedback that says "option C".”

Against: none found

### Name an organiser by what it asks

Don't give learners a branded name for a graphic organiser to remember; name it by its function and say what it asks in plain question language. One unit resolved a branded organiser name by calling the tool a plain five-part chart.

Curriculum feedback on a grade 3 reading course found that naming an organiser with a branded frame name just gives students one more label to remember, and asked for plain questions about what it is for. The rule went into the course's generation contract, which bans branded frames; an authored grade 4 lesson still used one, and its unit's sign-off resolved it by naming the tool by its function (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/video/u1-course-plan-notes.md`, lines 278-280). Procedures are treated the other way: the course names them on purpose, as tools the child carries.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md` (189): “**Rule.** Do not give the child a label to remember. Say what the frame asks.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md` (191-193): “"Naming the organizer the 'Adaptation Frame' just gives students one more label to remember. Use plain question language instead ('what problem is the animal solving? how does it survive?')."”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/video/u1-course-plan-notes.md` (278-280): “Contract §A2.2 bans branded frames such as "the Adaptation Frame". The signoff resolved this for U1 by naming the tool by function, a plain five-part chart.”

Against: Procedures, unlike organisers, are named on purpose in the same course: "a child who has watched FIND four times without once hearing it called FIND has been given four demonstrations and no tool." (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/pedagogy-and-lesson-structure.md`, lines 343-345); and an authored grade 4 lesson used a named frame until its unit's sign-off renamed it (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md`, lines 198-199).

<sub>criterion · wording · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Name the behaviour, not the child

When a learner games a task, name the behaviour, never the learner, and explain any penalty at the end with the way back, never as a live scold. A whole set answered in under 10 seconds costs one point, but the running counter never goes negative while the child is still working.

A Grade 3 reading sample's points rubric sets how gaming is shown to a child: the speed-click message names the behaviour, not the child, saying the set was answered faster than anyone can read it, that points are for thinking time and that reading it again brings the points straight back, with no shaming word. The running counter never goes negative while the child is still working, because the penalty is something to explain at the end, not a live scold, and on the end card the negative is shown with its explanation directly beneath it. A total of zero says so kindly, with the way to earn points in the same breath. These are design decisions in one sample, with no outcome measured.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md` (152): “The speed-click message names the behaviour, not the child.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md` (148): “The running counter never goes negative while the child is still working.”

Against: none found

### Name the part each clue comes from

When a lesson cites evidence from a text told in parts, name the part and recall the moment by what happens, not by a position like 'the middle'.

Curriculum rulings of 3 July 2026 in the Grade 3 procedure spec, quoted in the reading course renderer, say every reference to story evidence names its part, such as 'In the second part…' or 'By the end of the story…', and never calls a part that is not the final one 'the last part' (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/pedagogy/modelling-moment-procedures.md`, line 866). The spec's routine for finding evidence recalls the moment to re-read as content, what happened and what the character was like, never as a position such as 'in the middle' (line 842). Both are design rules; nothing measured their effect.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/pedagogy/modelling-moment-procedures.md` (866): “every reference to story evidence names its part ('In the second part…', 'By the end of the story…'); never 'the last part' for a non-final part.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/pedagogy/modelling-moment-procedures.md` (842): “I remember [the moment, recalled as CONTENT - what happened, what the character was like - never as a position like 'in the middle'].”

Against: none found

### Name the right part, ask for the rest

When an answer is partly right, say which part is right and ask only for the missing part.

A built Grade 3 lesson's feedback on a half-answer to a two-part question names the part the child gave and asks only for the missing one. A research-based conversation table for early reading makes the same move on a partly right answer: it confirms the part that is right, points to where to look, and asks what is missing.

Quoted:

### Never pass AI classmates off as peers

Don't present AI classmates as real peers, since learners got better at unmasking them through social and contextual inconsistency rather than language.

Notes on AI classmates cite the one study measuring this directly for school-age students in a live multi-agent setting: detection accuracy improved with repeated exposure, and learners moved from analysing language patterns early to shared social and contextual signals later (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/idea-02-ai-classmates-multi-agent-debate.md`, lines 119-123). The notes recommend designing for social and contextual consistency across repeated sessions, giving each voice a stable memory of earlier sessions (line 141). The project's synthesis adds that AI classmates should take the questioning half of productive talk and not be marketed as real peers, since visual style alone won't stop the fake read (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/IDEA-SYNTHESIS.md`, line 19).

Quoted:

- `g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/idea-02-ai-classmates-multi-agent-debate.md` (120-122): “detection accuracy improved with repeated exposure, and the strategy shifted from analyzing language patterns early to using shared social/contextual signals later”
- `g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/IDEA-SYNTHESIS.md` (19): “Use AI classmates for the **questioning** half of productive talk; don't market them as real peers.”
- `g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/IDEA-SYNTHESIS.md` (19): “Children get better at unmasking AI classmates over sessions, from **social and contextual inconsistency** rather than language (2026 DoppelBot study).”
- `g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/idea-02-ai-classmates-multi-agent-debate.md` (141): “give each expert a stable memory of prior sessions, since that is specifically what let students unmask AI agents with exposure.”

Published: [Schumacher et al. 2026, Detecting AI Impostors (DoppelBot)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30948)

Against: The evidence is one field study, with middle-school students rather than Grade 9 (`g9/library/collections/g3-repo-initiative-g9-othello-2026-10-01/research/idea-02-ai-classmates-multi-agent-debate.md`, lines 119-123). That study used text and game interaction, not synchronised voice and image (lines 152-153).

### Never say earlier work doesn't count

Never tell learners that the hard part is only now beginning or that what they have done so far does not count.

A Grade 3 reading course's published copy rules, distilled from review feedback, include one headed 'Do not demotivate': no line may tell a child that the hard part is only now beginning, or that what they have done so far does not count. It applies to every child-facing string and is checked by judgement, by a person or an agent, not by a validator.

### Never show a learner their level

Never show a learner an ability label, tier, group, colour or level name; keep any grouping, levelling and pacing inside the system.

The grade-3 rulebook's register with the child forbids exposing an ability label, tier, colour, group or level, beside the rule to talk to the child as a capable reader. A reading quality canon bars any persistent, child-visible ability tier, level or group label and keeps levelling in the engine, quoting a child who said 'I was orange, and I knew I couldn't read'.

### No adult punctuation for young readers

Keep adult punctuation and shorthand, such as em dashes, semicolons, any brackets, slash compounds and all-caps, out of young readers' text outside quoted source, and check it in code. The ban covers punctuation that reads as adult prose to an eight- or nine-year-old, with a short list of acronyms exempt from the caps rule.

A review comment on a grade 3 reading course found that machine-written em dashes made sentences harder for a child to read, and the course banned them from child-facing text. It extended the ban to semicolons, parentheses, slash compounds and all-caps emphasis, as punctuation that reads as adult prose to an eight- or nine-year-old, enforced by validators on lesson data and on text built into components (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md`, lines 18-38). The course's rule pack calls these house rules, not universal ones, and its style validator keeps semicolons only inside verified excerpts from source texts.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md` (10-12): “"AI uses loads of em dashes. I would remove them entirely. It is much better for a child to see other types of punctuation and they make sentences harder to read in my opinion.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md` (25-26): “**Rule.** Same ban, same reason, extended to the punctuation that reads as adult prose to an eight- or nine-year-old.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/content-and-copy.md` (18-20): “Enforced by `scripts/validate-contracts.mjs` for JSON content (em dash and en dash) and by `scripts/validate-component-text.mjs` for text hardcoded in components”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/authoring/inputs/rule-packs/reading-g3.json` (199): “These are GRADE-3-READING house rules, not universal ones, and that is the whole reason they live in a rule pack.”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/content/sources/README.md` (9): “The style validator preserves semicolons only inside those verified excerpts or verse lines”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (13): “The six hard-won content rules (each has a gate; violations WILL bounce)”

### No spoilers of what comes later

Nothing in a lesson may preview or point to what the student hasn't reached yet, though a teaser that gives away no events is allowed.

The reading course's checklist has a blocking row against any text, feedback or check that previews content the student has not reached, hunted across every node type (`grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md`, line 47). A content review of a reference lesson rejected an opening picture that gave away what would happen (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-aladdin-2026-08-25/feedback-text.txt`, line 6), and an external audit flagged a modelled inference that stated an event before the story reached it (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-inceptbench-2026-09-30/REGISTER.md`, line 85). A draft film plan keeps later events and the ending out of the opening film (`grade3-reading/artifacts/peter-pan-standing-2026-09-11/video-2026-09-16/PETER-PAN-PART-1-FILM.md`, line 79). The rule covers a lesson's text, feedback, checks, pictures and opening film.

Against: none found; the same review allows a teaser, "no spoilers of events but teasers" (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-aladdin-2026-08-25/feedback-text.txt`, line 4). The same build broke it on its first page: its first walkthrough answers a wrong tap on the first-read check with where that answer lives, "the river comes later, at the end of his wander…", and its table walkthrough of the same day still describes that reply as telling "where that answer actually lives". A later run found: A Grade 9 unit's opening film previews the ending on purpose, stating the killing as fact before any reading (`g9/sources/Othello Unit Video_ Info Sheet for Devfactory.md`). A later run found: A Grade 9 unit's reading texts feed forward before each section, and some feeds tell the learner what the section contains before it is read, which this rule forbids; the two courses' designs disagree (`g9/sources/Act 1 reading texts/Act 1/1. 1.1 - Iago_s Introduction - Text.docx`). A later run found: a Grade 9 unit film, played before the first reading, tells students on purpose where the story starts and where it ends, so every lesson reads as a step toward the ending (`othello-archive/sources/act1/Othello Unit Video - Info Sheet for Devfactory.txt`). A later run found: a Grade 9 unit-film brief deliberately tells the ending before the first reading, planting the destination and the start so that every lesson is read as a step on that journey (`g9/library/text/Lbde498.txt`). A later run found counter-evidence (`g9/script/v03/Othello-Unit-Video-Script-v3.txt`). A later run found counter-evidence (`othello-archive/sources/act1/Othello Unit Video - Info Sheet for Devfactory.txt`). A later run found counter-evidence (`g9/library/text/Lbde498.txt`). A later run found counter-evidence (`g9/film/TREATMENT.md`).

<sub>criterion · sequence, integrity, media · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### No two buttons for one job

Give each screen one forward button, and never show two buttons that do the same job. A screenshot sweep flags any screen showing more than 1 filled button.

Review feedback on a 3D version of the Grade 9 lessons rejected clutter and two controls doing one job, and its rules say no two controls may do the same job (`othello-archive/DESIGN.md`, line 4; `othello-archive/README.md`, line 87). Its shell keeps one filled Next in the footer as the only forward control, which no step may duplicate, with one filled button at a time (`othello-archive/BRIEF.md`, lines 12-13), and a screenshot sweep flags any screen with more than one filled button (`othello-archive/scripts/sweep.mjs`, line 69).

Quoted:

- `othello-archive/README.md` (87): “Keep the screen uncluttered. No two controls may do the same job.”
- `othello-archive/DESIGN.md` (4): “They reject clutter, repeated content, AI-sounding copy, and two controls doing one job.”
- `othello-archive/BRIEF.md` (12): “**Never render your own Next/Continue-to-next-step button.** The footer Next is the only forward control.”
- `othello-archive/BRIEF.md` (13): “**One filled button at a time.**”
- `othello-archive/scripts/sweep.mjs` (69): “r.filled.length > 1 && `${r.filled.length} filled buttons: ${r.filled.join(' | ')}`”

Against: The rule bars a second button, not a second way to press the one there is: in one step a click on the stage and the keyboard do the footer button's job (`othello-archive/src/steps/Explosion.tsx`, line 48).

<sub>criterion · format, accessibility · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Nothing gives the answer first

Nothing a student sees or hears may give the answer before the first attempt, including the picture behind a paused question.

The film's method: nothing a student sees or hears may give an answer before the first attempt; checks sit after the
scene they ask about, and the film is blurred while a question is on screen
(`othello-unit-video/research/METHODS.md`). The blur came after two checks paused over plates that showed their answer
(`g9/film/v06/README.md`), and the kit's rule counts the question's own wording too
(`othello-unit-video/.claude/skills/uvk-checks-make/SKILL.md`). A meta-analysis found learning impeded when a video is
not paused for interaction (Ploetzner 2024, https://doi.org/10.1080/10494820.2022.2123002). In the lesson engine,
nothing a student needs to work out is on screen before they answer; a word card that showed the meaning before the
question, and a delivery choice that showed what the speaker wants before the pick, were both fixed
(`g9/transformer/src/glue/animated.js`; `g9/transformer/v02/QA.md`, "Found and fixed").

Published: [Ploetzner 2024](https://doi.org/10.1080/10494820.2022.2123002)

Against: one build's exported
lesson still carries the key in its data (`g9/transformer/src/glue/animated.js`), and by the kit's own rule a check asks
only about what the film has just said, so it tests what was just heard. A later run found counter-evidence (`icloud-work/inceptbench-main/src/inceptbench/prompts/question/closed_ended_evaluation.txt`). A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/artifacts/peter-pan-standing-2026-09-11/ROUND-RESULT.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/video/u1-intro-audit.md`). The validator fails only an order identical to the key (`grade3-reading/course-renderer/scripts/validate-contracts.mjs`, line 823), so an order one swap from the answer still passes it.

<sub>criterion · integrity, media · universal · from g3, g9, research · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-2-round-1](../research/2026-10-12-2-round-1/), [2026-10-12-3-round-2](../research/2026-10-12-3-round-2/), [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### One next step, never a list

When feedback on writing finds several gaps, give one next step, on the earliest missing part in the order the writing builds, never a list or a chained second step.

A 3D version's paragraph marker returns one next step, never a list, and never writes the student's sentences for them. When several of the four jobs are missing it picks the earliest in point, evidence, analysis, link order, is told not to chain a second step with 'then' or 'and also', and code drops a chained second instruction if the model adds one anyway.

Quoted:

- `othello-archive/server/tasks/practice.mjs` (4): “It returns ONE next step, never a list, and never writes the student's sentences for them.”
- `othello-archive/server/tasks/practice.mjs` (53): “(if several jobs are missing, choose the earliest in point, evidence, analysis, link order). Do not chain a second step with 'then' or 'and also'.”
- `othello-archive/server/tasks/practice.mjs` (63): “one step only: drop a chained second instruction if the model adds one anyway”

Against: The code drops only a trailing clause opened by 'then', 'after that', 'also' or 'next' (`othello-archive/server/tasks/practice.mjs`, line 64), and nothing here measures whether one step helps more than a list.

<sub>criterion · feedback, hints · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### One plain voice for every line

Every line a student reads gets one plain voice: no praise, no exclamation marks, no verdict the screen already shows, and at most one question, none in a closing line.

Every line a student reads passes the same cleaner in code: no gushing, at most one question while a try remains and
none in a closing line, no semicolon or dash standing in for a comma, and no opening verdict, since the screen shows
the verdict itself (`g9/transformer/src/house.js`). The rules are a 3D version's reply cleaners and an animated
version's punctuation, used as they are. A test turns "Great job! You found the line. Which word matters? And why?"
into "You found the line. Which word matters?" (`g9/transformer/test/glue.test.mjs`). Before the rule, feedback opened
with "This is correct;" under the build's own YES (`g9/transformer/v02/QA.md`, "Found and fixed").

<sub>criterion · wording, feedback · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-2-round-1](../research/2026-10-12-2-round-1/), [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### One push-back in a debate

In a debate, push back once on the side the student took, only when the judge is sure of it, then offer a position they could defend, and mark nothing. The student answers one push-back that cites the text, and the task is never marked.

The lesson engine's debate block asks for a position and a reason, gives one push-back for the side taken, then offers a position the student could defend, and never marks any of it (`g9/transformer/web/lesson.js`; `g9/transformer/README.md`). Only a side the judge is sure of gets its push-back, which cites the text and which the student's next answer replies to. The rubric it exports to an animated version also asks the student to answer the other reading, saying why someone could see it the other way.

Quoted:

- `g9/transformer/web/lesson.js` (642): “Practice.tsx Evaluate: the question, a position and a reason; one push-back for the side taken, then a position”
- `g9/transformer/web/lesson.js` (663): “a side Jev is sure of gets its push-back, which the next answer replies to”
- `g9/transformer/README.md` (51): “says where they stand and why, answers one push-back that cites the text; never marked”
- `g9/transformer/src/glue/animated.js` (172): “Answers the other reading: says why someone could see it the other way, and answers them. One clause is enough.”
- `g9/transformer/src/glue/animated.js` (174): “You took a side, gave a reason from the text, and answered the other reading.”

Against: In an animated version's player the same debate is judged on three criteria over two tries, closing with "You took a side, gave a reason from the text, and answered the other reading." (`g9/transformer/src/glue/animated.js`, line 174).

<sub>criterion · feedback, format · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Pitch model answers at a strong student

Pitch a model answer at what a strong student of the grade could write in the time, not at expert writing. On the live proof, the judge's check that a model paragraph is something a strong Grade 9 student could write in about five minutes held with a confidence of 0.84 and 0.86.

The writer prompts of four blocks in the lesson engine pitch their model writing at Grade 9 students, three of them at a strong one, and the claim-evidence-reasoning prompt at what such a student could write in five minutes. Two blocks also ask the judge, as an advisory check, whether the model could come from such a student: one asks whether a strong one could write it in about five minutes, the other whether both paragraphs read like Grade 9 writing rather than an adult critic's. On the live proof the first check held at p 0.84 and 0.86.

Quoted:

- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/cer-frame.js` (61): “Is this model answer something a strong Grade 9 student could write in about five minutes?”
- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/cer-frame.js` (24): “could write in five minutes.”
- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/compare-paragraphs.js` (23): “answer the lesson question the way two Grade 9 students”
- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/compare-paragraphs.js` (62): “Do both paragraphs read like writing by Grade 9 students”
- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/debate.js` (52): “a position a strong Grade 9 student could defend, with a reason from the text.”
- `g9/transformer/src/mechanics/worked-model.js` (36): “how to answer the lesson question before the student writes, as a strong Grade 9”
- `g9/transformer/v01/proof-2026-10-09.json` (237-240): “"id": "grade-9", "required": false, "verdict": "holds", "p": 0.84”
- `g9/transformer/v01/proof-2026-10-09.json` (509-512): “"id": "grade-9", "required": false, "verdict": "holds", "p": 0.86”
- `g9/transformer/v01/proof-2026-10-09.json` (265-268): “"id": "student-voice", "required": false, "verdict": "unclear", "p": 0.59”

Against: Both judge checks are advisory and never hold a block: the drama comparison's student-voice check came back unclear at p 0.59 and the block shipped. Nobody has measured whether students find the pitch right.

<sub>criterion · wording, demand · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Plain short text where the learner acts

Keep the words a learner reads just before acting to a short plain instruction of at most 14 words, and put any framing in the guide's narration. A cap of 2 sentences and 14 words per field, enforced in code, caught the long task openers in two lessons with one rule.

A reading course's interface rules cap an interaction opener, the text a child reads right before acting, at 2 sentences and 14 words per field, with no theatrics: a plain 'Tap the clues that answer the question' rather than an excited announcement of a clue hunt. Framing belongs in the guide's narration, never on the surface where the child acts. The rule came after the same complaint was raised across several review rounds, and it became a check in code that caught the long openers in two lessons with one rule.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (28-30): “**An interaction opener is capped at 2 sentences / 14 words per field.** The text a child reads right before ACTING (a hunt's invitation/opening, and by extension any tap-now surface) carries no theatrics”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/05-UI-UX-PRINCIPLES.md` (32): “Framing belongs in the guide's narration, never on the acting surface.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/calibration/ROUNDS.md` (64-65): “clue-hunt-opener length class (now a red-controlled 2-sentence/14-word cap on `hunt.open`, one rule catching both the L4 and L5 instances)”

Against: none found

<sub>criterion · wording, stem · domain · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Plain, consistent words for each step

Name each step and concept one way everywhere, in plain words for what a reader thinks or does, with no internal jargon or stock assistant phrasing. Complaints about the wording of taught steps, stems and instructions came back in 4 later review sections, more than any other class.

A recurrence gauge over seven review records of the reading course, 183 classified entries with 54 reported defects, found that complaints about whether named steps, sentence stems and instructions were consistent came back in 4 later sections after the first review raised them, more than any other class (`grade3-reading/artifacts/codex-qa/experience-2026-09-14/recurrence/CLASSIFICATION-REVIEW-v3.md`, `grade3-reading/codex/qa-recurrence.json`). One of them asked for no machine-speak such as "run all four" (`grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-aladdin-2026-08-25/REGISTER.md`). The course's checklist bans procedure jargon and coined names in what children read, and sweeps stock assistant phrasing, such as the guide narrating its own mechanism or restating what a button says, as its own class, since no validator caught it (`grade3-reading/artifacts/AlphaRead-QC-Checklist-v2-2026-09-04.md`). Use the same sentence stems throughout. Make a frame's wording match the procedure it teaches, and check that wording as a class of its own.

<sub>criterion · wording · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/), [2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas](../research/2026-10-12-6-transformer-ideas/)</sub>

### Point only at steps that exist

Every hint, recap or feedback line that points to another step must name a step that exists, by its current name, and say what that step actually did. In one audit, three wrong-option feedback lines used a step name the lesson had retired.

A curriculum-adherence report on four Grade 3 lessons found a build frame whose hint told students to use one of the words they had locked in at Step 3, a step that did not exist in the served lesson, and a recap meant to mirror the model's thinking whose text described a different task. An external audit of the same lessons found three wrong-option feedback lines using a step name the lesson had retired.

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/curriculum-adherence-2026-09-24/REPORT.md` (98): “but the text describes the Hook task, not the Lost Boys model”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/feedback-inceptbench-2026-09-30/REGISTER.md` (115): “Three wrong-option feedback lines use "Zoom in", the retired step-name”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/content/U1-L6/AUTHORING-NOTES.md` (675-676): “The missing evidence pool was never the unit's prose. It is the unit's earlier CHARTS.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/content/U1-L6/AUTHORING-NOTES.md` (680): “Every tale a hinge recap row names must have been charted in an”

- `grade3-reading/.claude/skills/pre-weekend-audit/SKILL.md` (44): “Cross-lesson promises: receipts' next_title, forward references that must pay off.”

Against: none found

<sub>criterion · alignment, hints, feedback · universal · from g3 · measured · held · found in [2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep](../research/2026-10-12-4-deep-sweep/), [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Point only at what is on screen

Never point anyone reading or marking an item at what they cannot see, like an off-screen label, code, highlight or line, or the brief and source facts behind it. On about 28 word cards, the second hint told a student to reread a line they had not met and could not see.

A review of the reading course found a question and its feedback citing a "Source C" that no passage, tag or heading in the lesson ever named, a label leaked from the source material, and internal span codes in a modelled think-aloud that nothing on the child's screen carried. The next review found that on about 28 word cards, which come before any passage and show no text, the second hint told the student to reread a line they had not met, and that nine hints sent students to reread a highlight that was never shown, because the highlighting data went only to the scorer.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/g5-course/ADVERSARIAL-REVIEW-2026-08-19-R2.md` (110): “is ever highlighted.** `grounding` is scorer-only (verified: `SpeakGate`/`ScrItem`”
- `icloud-work/ap-social-studies-generator/python/benchmark_runs/analysis_iter2.md` (42): “Part (a) scoring is problematic: it references 'the brief'”

### Points for focused time, not answers

Give points once at the finish, for whole focused minutes and a passed checkpoint, never for each correct answer. One contract gives 1 point per whole focused minute at a 90% checkpoint, and none per answer.

A reading app's points code was found inventing quiz points at three per correct answer, with no 80% floor and no link to time; the fix recorded only raw correctness and left points to the platform's gradebook, where the only points shown are time-based. A later release contract for a Grade 5 course gives no per-answer points: a correct answer records progress and feedback, and points are counted once at a successful finish from whole focused minutes, one per minute, if the finish avoids the gaming condition and meets a 90 percent checkpoint. The sources state the rule but give no reason for it tied to learning.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0060-incept-read-app-field-notebook.qmd` (52-53): “The broker previously **fabricated** quiz XP (`correct × 3`, no 80% floor, not time-based”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0060-incept-read-app-field-notebook.qmd` (53-54): “Now it records only raw correctness/accuracy and lets TimeBack's gradebook own XP; the only XP surfaced (Caliper `xpEarned`) is time-based.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/docs/G5_STUDENT_EXPERIENCE_RELEASE_GATE.qmd` (37): “A correct response records immediate progress and feedback; it does not award per-answer XP.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/docs/G5_STUDENT_EXPERIENCE_RELEASE_GATE.qmd` (38-40): “Preview XP is calculated once at a successful lesson finish from whole focused minutes at one XP per minute. The finish must resolve required evidence and repairs, avoid the gaming condition, and meet the 90 percent preview checkpoint.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0174-august-consolidation-and-scout-lesson-feedback.qmd` (31-32): “no per-answer XP; preview XP once at finish; 1 XP per whole focused minute; 90% checkpoint”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/docs/G5_STUDENT_EXPERIENCE_RELEASE_GATE.qmd` (21): “a correct response changed progress but appeared to earn no XP;”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/docs/G5_STUDENT_EXPERIENCE_RELEASE_GATE.qmd` (44-46): “The interface must state that distinction before completion, then show the completion result, awarded Preview XP when nonzero, and the next unlocked lesson without implying that one correct answer should mint XP.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md` (99): “paying for a partial set is farmable in a way that is easy to”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md` (100): “miss: answer one question correctly — now the child is at 100% and clears the floor — then never”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (938): “test exercises the happy path fully, a precondition that only matters on partial input is invisible”
- `grade3-reading/incept-read-app/XP-COMPLIANCE-AUDIT.md` (78-79): “XP is awarded for ANY correct answer — `1/10` correct still writes `3 XP`. The rule says NO XP below 80% accuracy.”

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md` (33): “pays a small, fixed amount for each teaching step the child finishes.”

Against: The same contract lists a correct response that appeared to earn no points among the failures that prompted it, and answers by stating the rule on screen rather than changing it. A later run found counter-evidence (`grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/XP-RUBRIC.md`). A later run found counter-evidence (`othello-archive/BRIEF.md`).

### Progress a child can read

Show progress as a labelled count a child can read, such as 'Page X of Y', and name a page's internal steps rather than present them as page progress. A row of about 28 page dots with no legend did not move across five content steps.

A supplied UI review found a progress row of about 28 dots, too fine to count and with no legend, that did not advance across five content steps, and suggested a simple count instead (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md`, line 21). The project found that its dots counted pages, not the steps inside a page, and replaced them with an explicit 'Page X of Y' plus named step text, never calling internal steps page progress (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md`, line 11). An independent source review accepted that page number and page purpose stay visible (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/NAVIGATION-INDEPENDENT-REVIEW-by-residual.md`, line 16).

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (21): “It did not advance across five content steps on `/p2`.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/aladdin-p1-ui-review.reference.md` (21): “It uses about 28 dots, too fine to count and far below a usable touch size, in three shapes and colours with no legend.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md` (11): “Existing dots represent pages, not the substeps of vocabulary/model.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md` (11): “Replace in PeterPan with explicit Page X of Y and keep local named step text. Never call internal steps page progress.”
- `grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/NAVIGATION-INDEPENDENT-REVIEW-by-residual.md` (16): “Page number plus page purpose remain visible; Back sits beside the primary action.”

Against: The mapped change is marked "Scoped source changed; browser acceptance pending" (`grade3-reading/ui-audit-2026-09-08/gold-standard/REVIEW-MAPPING.md`, line 11).

### Promise only what later lessons keep

A lesson's promises about what comes next must hold in the lessons that follow; reword one they don't keep as something the learner will do. Each of three lessons' closing words was used 57 to 59 times in its own lesson and 0 times in the lessons after.

A simulated review round found that three lessons' closing screens promise the lesson's word will come along, and counts showed the words used 59, 57 and 57 times in their own lessons and 0 times in the lessons after; approved reference lessons showed the same shape. An earlier smoke audit had checked each 'next time' line against the following lesson and found them true, though one automated check wrongly assumed the last lesson in scope had no next lesson. The round's clean fix is to reword the line so it promises the learner will keep using the word, which is true and within the lesson's control.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-2026-09-15.md` (89): “Each lesson's closing receipt explicitly promises the target word will continue ('Your word is coming with us: <word>')”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-2026-09-15.md` (90): “That single-word, 0%-recurrence shape is the exact shape the finding calls out in L4 (enchanting, 59 in L4, 0 in L5-L7), L5 (menacing, 57 in L5, 0 after) and L6 (merciless, 57 in L6, 0 in L7)”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-2026-09-15.md` (95): “the clean version is to reword the receipt so it promises the child will keep using the word, which is true and within our control, instead of implying the story will repeat it.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/REGISTER-2026-09-15.md` (95): “It is not a stem, an option, an answer or any scored interaction, so no student ever gets a question wrong or a rail blocked because of it”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PRE-WEEKEND-SMOKE-2026-08-28.md` (131): “**Forward references in wrap text**, verified true of the following lesson:”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/arc-audit/PRE-WEEKEND-SMOKE-2026-08-28.md` (181-182): “an automated check assumed the last lesson in scope should have no forward pointer.”

Against: It was kept as accepted exposure, partly because approved reference lessons do the same and the line "is not a stem, an option, an answer or any scored interaction, so no student ever gets a question wrong or a rail blocked because of it" (REGISTER-2026-09-15.md line 95).

### Purpose, first action and success up front

Before work begins, show the learner its purpose, the first action and what success will look like, in words the learner can read, not only in review documents.

The golden student-course standard asks of every lesson whether the intended outcome is observable and clear to the learner. It accepts a lesson when one declared operation, a learner-readable purpose, the first action and the success evidence are visible before work begins, and counts as failures a generic skill label, several competing goals, a hidden first action and success described only to the people reviewing it. Its claim boundary adds that plain language must keep the true cognitive demand. In one course's preflight, the simulated student's first probe asks for the same before the first action: what to do, what information matters and what success will look like.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/quality/golden-student-course/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (277): “Is the intended outcome observable and clear to the learner?”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/quality/golden-student-course/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (282): “One declared operation, learner-readable purpose, first action, and success evidence are visible before work begins.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/quality/golden-student-course/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (283): “Generic skill label; several competing goals; hidden first action; success described only to reviewers.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/quality/golden-student-course/versions/1.0.0/STANDARD.qmd` (284): “Plain language must retain the true cognitive demand.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/quality/adversarial-preflight/characters/grade-5-us-student/probes.json` (8): “Explain what to do, what information matters, and what success will look like before the first action.”

Against: none found

### Quick for facts, explained for concepts

Give feedback at once on simple facts, and on concepts explain why an answer is right or wrong.

The delivery platform's learning-science page lists feedback among the force multipliers that work once the foundations are in place: immediate for basic facts, elaborated, explaining why, for concepts. Its principles page carries the same line, and the reading project took both as its bar, so this is a rule adopted from the platform, not a result measured here.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0027-learning-science.qmd` (38): “| **Feedback**        | Immediate for basic facts; elaborated (explaining why) for concepts.   |”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/knowledge/kb-0032-principles.qmd` (65): “| **Feedback** | Immediate for basic facts; elaborated (explaining why) for concepts. |”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/HOTBUCKET.md` (283): “Long feedback text is itself a failure for Grade 3.”
- `grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/handoffs/g3-reading-v2-feedback-2026-07-16/tooling/ti-courseplans-2169d1f/scripts/lane-tools/op-lint/README.md` (15): “feedback must carry a `misconception_id` and be <= 60 words (hard cap); 41-60 words emits a non-blocking warning (40 is the standard's target).”
- `grade3-reading/course-renderer/docs/rules/questions-and-assessment.md` (367-369): “A criterion is the better hint of the two when the child's error is about a kind of thinking rather than a missed fact, because it transfers to the next item and a page reference does not.”

Against: Explained feedback still has to be short for young readers: long feedback text was itself counted a failure for Grade 3 (`grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/HOTBUCKET.md`, line 283), and a lesson lint caps feedback at 60 words, with 40 as the target (`grade3-reading/knowledge_bot/handoffs/g3-reading-v2-feedback-2026-07-16/tooling/ti-courseplans-2169d1f/scripts/lane-tools/op-lint/README.md`, line 15).

### Redirect a wrong prior-knowledge answer

Before reading, meet a wrong prior-knowledge answer with 'find a connection, then let's go back and look', never 'that's wrong', and judge only that the answer is real.

A reading quality canon meets a wrong answer drawn from prior knowledge with 'find a connection, then let's go back and look', never a bare 'that's wrong'. The grade-3 rulebook's scoring layer has pre-reading beats judge only whether the child said something real: no passage is sent, no vocabulary is required and nothing is to be cited.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/artifacts/u4l2-build/g5-audit/P2-RULESET.md` (466): “A wrong prior-knowledge answer is met with 'find a connection, then let's go back and look', never 'that's wrong'.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/u4l2-build/g5-audit/P2-RULESET.md` (467): “Wrong prior-knowledge answers get redirection, never a bare 'that's wrong'.”
- `grade3-reading/artifacts/judgment-corpus/distilled-src/03-INSTRUCTION-DESIGN.md` (262-263): “Pre-reading beats judge only whether the child said something real — no passage sent, no vocabulary required, nothing to cite.”

Against: none found

<sub>criterion · feedback, marking · universal · from g3 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Reveal one strong reading, not the answer

For an interpretive question, show a model answer only after a committed attempt, labelled as one strong reading with an invitation to disagree, never as the answer.

The Grade 9 research brief combines a guarded-chatbot trial, a structured AI tutoring trial and a study of autonomy-supporting behaviour into an explicit hint ladder: a question back to the student, a pointer to a paragraph, two candidate sentences, then the model answer only after a committed attempt, labelled 'one strong reading', with an invitation to disagree. The Grade 9 strategy adopts the ladder for its argument tasks. Its order from general to specific matches the rule that help comes after a try, cues first; what it adds is how an interpretive item's answer is shown.

Quoted:

- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md` (182): “Set the hint ladder as an explicit rule: (1) a question back to the student, (2) point to a paragraph, (3) narrow to two candidate sentences, (4) show the model answer only after a committed attempt, labeled "one strong reading," with an invitation to disagree.”
- `othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/strategy/grade-9-strategy.md` (144): “**Hint ladder (from Bastani et al.):** a question back, then a pointer to a paragraph, then two candidate lines, then "one strong reading" only after a committed attempt, with an invitation to disagree.”

Published: [Bastani, Bastani, Sungu, Ge, Kabakcı & Mariman 2025](https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2422633122)

Against: It is a proposal, neither built nor piloted, and in the trial it draws on, the hint-only arm erased most of the harm but did not beat the control group on the exam (`othello-course-renderer-draft/repo/docs/design/grade-9/research/learners-voice-guide.md`, line 164).

<sub>criterion · feedback, key, hints · universal · from g9 · adopted-rule · held · found in [2026-10-12-5-full-crawl](../research/2026-10-12-5-full-crawl/)</sub>

### Reveal the unsure route after a delay

Reveal an 'I'm not sure' route to help after a learner has been stuck a while, because shown up front the choice that can't be wrong becomes the cheap path. One reading build reveals the route after about 20 seconds, below the options.

Every lesson in a Grade 3 reading build authored a branch for the child who freezes, with its own gentler turns, but none of it could run: the player classified only taps, so a child with no answer had to guess and got the wrong conversation. A quiet 'I'm not sure yet' door now routes there, revealed after about 20 seconds below the options. The build's rules give the reason for the delay: it is the one button on screen that cannot be wrong, so mounted with the options it becomes the cheap path and children who would have answered take it. Because the door is mounted on every check, a lesson missing the branch gives it a dead end, which the rules count as a defect.

Quoted:

- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (831): “An authored branch nothing can reach is not a feature, it is a file.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (839): “An escape hatch offered up front stops being an escape hatch.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (840): “~20s (`CFU_UNSURE_MS`), below the tappables, deliberately.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (840-842): “It is the one button on screen that cannot be wrong, so mounted with the options it becomes the *cheap path* and children who would have answered take it.”
- `grade3-reading/edullm-review-hub/course-packages/archive/g3-reading-v4/content-for-analysis/BUILD-RULES.md` (845-846): “A lesson missing the branch therefore has a door onto a **dead end**, found by the freezing child.”

Against: none found
